# Ayran CsDrive WebHost

The Tauri app in `csdrive-webhost-tauriapp` has exactly one frontend, the **admin-app** — a React/Vite project living at the root of that same folder (`src/`, `index.html`, `package.json`, `vite.config.ts`, with the Rust backend in `src-tauri/`). It is the Tauri app's own compiled-in frontend (`frontendDist` in `src-tauri/tauri.conf.json`, embedded into the binary and so into every installer), it always occupies the main window, and nothing — neither the user nor a web app — can replace it. It manages a machine-wide *data folder* (below) holding an `admin` folder for the app's own database and secrets, a `user` folder meant purely for arbitrary user-authored content, and a `files` folder for the Notes app's cache. The user can write their own `.html` files anywhere under `user` (e.g. `user/index.html`, `user/myapp/index.html`), but those are only ever opened as *secondary* windows (via the admin-app's own Files tab or User Apps tab — "Open as web app"/"add a new window entry"). Besides those *user apps* the app ships **system apps** — more pages of the same compiled-in frontend, **Notes** being the first — which are opened the same way and managed in the System Apps tab (see "System apps and Notes" below).

## Folder layout

**Where all of this lives is defined in exactly one place: `csdrive-webhost-tauriapp/src-tauri/src/layout.rs`** — `DEFAULT_DATA_FOLDER` (the default data folder; `%APPDATA%\com.ayran.csdrive-webhost-tauriapp` for a release build today — see the debug/release split just below), the `admin`/`user`/`files` folder names (and inside `files`: `a`, `b`, `c`, the thumbnails `t` and `tb`, and `data.db`, see "The Filen cache" and "Media"), `FILEN_SESSIONS_FILE`, and `ADMIN_APP_ID` (the admin-app's key for its saved UI state). Nothing else hardcodes them: the backend, the keychain entry names (the last component of `DEFAULT_DATA_FOLDER`), the `seed_demo_data` example (`#[path]`-includes that file) and the admin-app (which names the user folder as the root `user`, never by path) all derive from it. To run the app **completely isolated** from real data (e.g. for tests), set `DEFAULT_DATA_FOLDER` to a scratch absolute path (`r"C:\Temp\csdrive-isolated-test"`) and rebuild: the run gets its own data folder, database and keychain entries, and can't reach the real one. (The WebView2 browser-storage profile is still shared, as it is keyed by the app identifier; the app keeps no state there.) Remember to set it back. **The person uses the custom data folder (Settings → "Change data folder") for the app they really use on this machine, and its pointer file (`data-location.enc`) sits in the *default* data folder — the very place a test run reads — so a run that is not isolated opens the production data, reads and writes.** Never run tests, the demo seeder or "Change data folder" / "Delete app data" against it: isolate the run first (above), and if the isolation isn't in force, stop and say so rather than carry on (see `README.md`).

**A debug build gets its own, separate default data folder — `com.ayran.csdrive-webhost-tauriapp-debug`, via `#[cfg(debug_assertions)]` — so `cargo run`/`cargo tauri dev` never shares a data folder, `data.db` or keychain entries with an installed release build, and both can run genuinely at once.** Asked for directly ("a way to run the debug app without closing the production app"): before this, both build kinds read the one same constant, so a debug and a release process running together would have fought over the very same `data.db`/`filen-sessions.enc` — two processes genuinely writing the same SQLite file concurrently, not merely risky. `cfg(debug_assertions)` needed no new build flag or feature — it's true for `cargo build`/`cargo run`, false for `--release` and for `cargo tauri build`'s own release profile, so the ordinary debug/release split already decides it. **The accepted consequence, flagged by the person before this was built, not found after**: anything only ever reachable from a debug run before now — in particular, a Filen account logged into from a `cargo run` session — lived in what was, until this change, the *one shared* default folder; a debug run from now on starts fresh in its own folder and won't see it, the same as it wouldn't see anything living only in the release folder. Logging in again, once, under the new debug folder is expected. The data-folder-relocation pointer file already lives *inside* whichever default folder is active (`data_location.rs`'s `config_file_path`), so a custom folder set from a release build and one set from a debug build were already two completely independent pointers — unaffected by this change either way.

**The WebView2 *browser-storage* profile is still shared between a debug and a release run** — confirmed live by reading the real `--user-data-dir` argument off both running `msedgewebview2.exe` processes (`Get-CimInstance Win32_Process`): both pointed at the identical `%LOCALAPPDATA%\com.ayran.csdrive-webhost-tauriapp\EBWebView`, because that path is keyed by the Tauri `identifier` in `tauri.conf.json` (unchanged, deliberately shared), never by `DEFAULT_DATA_FOLDER`. This is the same, already-documented characteristic noted two paragraphs up ("the app keeps no state there") — not a new gap this change introduces, and not something either process's own data (which *is* now fully separate) depends on. One practical consequence: a `WEBVIEW2_ADDITIONAL_BROWSER_ARGUMENTS` CDP-debugging flag only takes effect on that shared browser host's *first* creation for the identifier — if a release instance is launched first with no such flag, a debug instance launched afterward with its own `--remote-debugging-port` gets no CDP access at all, since it's handed the browser host the release instance already created. Live-tested (isolated from production — the installed release app launched plainly, a freshly built debug binary launched alongside it, `tasklist` showing two distinct PIDs with no crash and no lock conflict): both wrote their own separate `admin/data.db` with no I/O contention, confirming the folder split itself is the thing that actually matters, and the shared WebView2 profile is cosmetic. **Separate taskbar icons**: yes — confirmed via `Get-Process` that the two run as genuinely different OS processes with different `Path`s (the installed release `.exe` vs. `target\debug\...\.exe`) and different, non-zero `MainWindowHandle`s, and via a source search across the whole Tauri/wry/tao dependency stack (`tao`, `wry`, `tauri-runtime-wry`) that nothing in it ever sets an explicit Windows `AppUserModelID` — so Windows falls back to its documented default of grouping taskbar buttons by the owning process's own executable path, which differs between the two. (A literal screenshot of the taskbar itself wasn't obtainable in this environment — `CopyFromScreen` captured only the desktop wallpaper, not a composited interactive desktop — so this rests on the process/path evidence above, not a direct visual check.)

The folders sit directly under the data folder (the default above, or wherever Settings' "Change data folder" points):
- `admin/` — the app's own files, none of which is ever served to a window: `data.db` (this app's SQLite database — secondary windows/tab groups/tabs/tags, the generic `app_state` table, the `global_settings` table, remembered device folders, connected Filen accounts; see `secondary_windows::init_db`) and `filen-sessions.enc` (below). The admin-app itself is **not** in the data folder: it's the Tauri app's compiled-in frontend, so there is no `admin/dist` and nothing on disk to tamper with.
- `user/` — arbitrary user-authored `.html`/`.js`/`.css`/etc., served to secondary windows via the `csuser://` custom protocol. The backend never reads anything from here except in response to the admin-app explicitly asking to open/list something in it.

- `files/` — the Notes app's cache of Filen accounts and their branches, and the branches and thumbnails of the folders of this device (see "The Filen cache", "Branches of a folder of this device" and "Media"). **Everything directly inside `files/` is one of: `a` and `data.db`** — nothing else lives at the top level. `a` holds one `NNN` folder pair per account or per folder of this device that has branches or thumbnails; **inside an account's or a root's own `NNN`**: `c` (its cached content — accounts only), `tmp` (an account's uploads in flight), `b` (its branches), `t` (its thumbnails) and `tb` (its branches' thumbnails) — each keeping the owner's own folder hierarchy. **`tmp`** (`a/NNN/tmp`) is where a Filen upload is assembled while it goes up — the pieces are written there as they are sent and the file *becomes the cached copy* when it finishes; anything left by an interrupted upload is deleted at the next start. `files/local-uploads` is the same idea for a file arriving from the device's file chooser into a *local* folder (`fs_upload.rs`): its pieces are appended there and the finished file is moved into the destination, so the person's own folders never hold a half-written file; it is emptied at every start. A Filen account is `NNN-filen@@<email>@@<id>` / `NNN`, a folder of this device `NNN-local-fs@@<root guid>` / `NNN`, both numbered together in `a`; a branch is `MMM-<name>` / `MMM` inside the owner's own `b` (and `tb`, for its thumbnails). Like `admin/`, it is a **protected path** (`data_location::protected_paths`): no file command or SQLite call can reach it, even inside a picked folder — only the `filen_cache_*` commands read and write it.

The three folders are created unconditionally at startup (`lib.rs`'s `.setup()`), regardless of which files inside them do or don't already exist. Whenever a folder needs a short name a program can rely on and a long one a person can read, the app uses **folder pairs** (`folder_pairs.rs`, strategy in `docs/strategies/folder-pairs-strategy.md`; `docs/strategies/` is where the app's strategies are written down, one `.md` each).

**The admin-app** is a React app (TypeScript, built by Vite into `dist/`, embedded by Tauri — see "Building and running"). It is a file manager for the `user` folder and any folders the user picks, a file manager for connected filen.io accounts (any number of them), a SQLite management studio for `.db` files in those places, the manager of the apps' windows/tabs/tags (the System Apps tab, first and the default, and the User Apps tab — the same component, `AppsTab kind="system" | "user"`), a browser-storage inspector, and the settings.

`csdrive-webhost-userapps/` holds source for small standalone apps that can be *deployed* — copied as a new folder of `.html`/etc. content into the `user` root — via the admin-app's own Files tab (see "Deploying apps" below), as opposed to the admin-app, which is the one privileged app: always built into the installer and always the main window.

## App state storage: data.db, not browser storage

An app's own UI-state persistence (active tab, last-browsed folder, collapsed groups, ...) belongs in the app-owned `data.db` (see the generic `app_state` table in `secondary_windows`'s crate — `get_app_state`/`set_app_state`), *not* IndexedDB or Web Storage. Reason: the data folder can be relocated (see the Settings tab / `data_location`) precisely so testing can switch between profiles, but browser storage lives in the fixed WebView2 profile rather than wherever the data folder currently points — so it never actually switched with the rest of the app's data. Rows are namespaced by an `app_id` — the relative path of the html file the window was opened from (from `user/`, for a secondary-window app; `system:<id>`, e.g. `system:notes`, for a system app; `layout::ADMIN_APP_ID` for the admin-app itself) — which the **backend** works out from the calling window itself (`get_app_state`/`set_app_state` take no app id; any one a caller sends is ignored), so an app can only ever reach its own state — so the same app keeps separate state when copied to more than one location on disk, and multiple apps sharing this one database never collide. This applies to every app in `csdrive-webhost-userapps/`, present and future; the admin-app's own `src/lib/appState.ts` is the reference implementation. It does not apply to browser storage a user explicitly creates through a generic inspection tool (e.g. admin-app's Storage tab) — those are arbitrary by design and named by the user, not the app, and have nothing to do with an app's own settings. If an app used browser storage for this before switching to `data.db`, it's fine to just abandon the old IndexedDB database/keys rather than migrate their contents, unless they hold data too valuable to lose.

**A paginated list's position is saved as the number of records skipped, never as a page number** (`src/lib/pagedPosition.ts`: `offsetOfPage`, `pageOfOffset`, `validOffset`). The page size is one setting for the whole app (below), so it can change between saving a position and restoring it, and "page 3" only means the same records for the same size; "50 records skipped" means the same records under any size — restored with a size of 10 the page shown is the 6th, which holds the record that was first on screen. Files, Filen.io and Notes save it with their place (`offset` in `filesTab.location` / `filenTab.location`, `o=` in a Notes tab's resource id and its last place), and **nothing is saved over a position until the listing it belongs to has loaded** (a `restoringPage` flag), or the page still saying 0 would overwrite it; a folder that can't be listed drops its position. A new paginated list that keeps its position does the same.

**Global settings** are the exception to "an app only reaches its own state": the `global_settings` table (`get_global_setting`/`set_global_setting`, granted to every window) holds the few preferences that are deliberately shared by every app — today the list **page size** (`listPageSize`, `src/lib/listPageSize.ts`), so paging 10 rows a page in the Files tab does so in Notes too. Keep it to genuinely global preferences.

**A notebook keeps its own page size, apart from that shared one** (asked for directly: "when in the scope of a notebook, let's persist [the page size] separately for each notebook"). `lib/listPageSize.ts`'s `getNotebookPageSize`/`setNotebookPageSize` are a second pair beside the global ones, stored in Notes' own `app_state` (never `global_settings` — a notebook is a Notes-only concept, not something Files/Filen.io have any business reading) as one map, `notes.pageSizeByNotebook`, keyed by the notebook's own `entryKey` (`notebooks.ts`: its *location* — `sourceId`/`folder`/`fileName` — not its GUID, for the same reason every other per-notebook lookup in this app already uses `entryKey`: two listed entries can share a GUID, but never a location). **Falls back to the shared page size, not the hardcoded default**, until the notebook has had one of its own actually set — the person's usual preference is the more useful first guess than an arbitrary 25, the same reasoning `getGlobalPageSize` itself already falls back to `DEFAULT_PAGE_SIZE` rather than erroring on a bad/missing value.

`NotesApp.tsx` (the File Manager, the one place the page-size `<select>` is actually reachable while browsing a notebook's folder tree) resolves which notebook the folder shown is inside — `notebookContaining(await loadNotebooks(), sourceId, path)`, the same helper and the same ad hoc "look it up fresh" pattern User Action's own `pathPicked` already uses — every time `sourceId`/`path` changes, kept in a ref (`notebookKeyRef`, not state: nothing renders from it directly, and `setPageSize` needs to read it synchronously at the moment of a change, not through a stale closure) rather than resolved once at mount; `pageSize` itself is re-resolved in the same pass. Changing the selector writes to whichever store `notebookKeyRef.current` names at that moment — the notebook's own, or the shared one outside every notebook — never both. **Skipped entirely for a scoped explorer** (`scope`, the Note Files Explorer): it has no notebook concept of its own to persist against, the same `!scope` guard `showLocation`'s own branch resolution already uses for the identical reason. `NotePage.tsx` (the notes list's own search results, which have no page-size selector of their own to change anything with) reads the same per-notebook value for consistency — from the `notebook` it already resolves in its own `load()`, so no second lookup was needed there.

**A real test-setup mistake, not a bug in the feature, cost the first live attempt**: `get_app_state`/`set_app_state` are namespaced per the *calling window's own app id* (`app_state.rs`/`appState.ts`'s own, already-documented rule) — registering the test notebook by invoking `set_app_state('notes.notebooks', …)` from the **admin** window (its app id is `layout::ADMIN_APP_ID`, not `system:notes`) wrote it into the admin's own, completely separate namespace, invisible to `loadNotebooks()` when the Notes window later read its *own* `notes.notebooks` — so `notebookContaining` correctly, faithfully returned `null` for a notebook the Notes window had genuinely never been told about, and the page-size change landed in the shared global store exactly as designed for "outside any notebook." Once the notebook was registered by invoking the same command *from the Notes window itself*, the rest resolved correctly. Verified live (isolated **debug** data folder — a scratch `DEFAULT_DATA_FOLDER`, reverted and confirmed by a clean `git diff` on `layout.rs` afterward — desktop, real CDP, through the real UI, not a hand-called backend function): a folder-based test notebook (`nb-pgsz-test`, one real file inside it so its listing isn't empty — `Pagination` renders nothing for `totalItems === 0`, which the first live attempt also tripped over before realizing it) with the global page size set to 50; inside the notebook, the selector read 50 (the fallback, nothing notebook-specific set yet); changing it to 10 through a real `<select>` + dispatched `change` event persisted `{"local:user|nb-pgsz-test|":10}` to `notes.pageSizeByNotebook` and left `listPageSize` at exactly `50`, untouched; navigating out to the plain user root via the real breadcrumb showed the selector back at `50` (the global value); navigating back into the notebook showed it at `10` again — the complete round trip, both directions. **Not separately exercised live**: `NotePage.tsx`'s own read-only consumption — it shares the identical `entryKey`/`getNotebookPageSize` call already proven correct above, with nothing of its own to get wrong differently, and there's no selector there to click through in the first place. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npm run build` both clean; no Rust change, so `cargo test --lib` doesn't apply.

**A second dimension on top of the notebook one: the *resource type* being listed** (asked for directly: "let's also persist [the page size] on per resource type being listed... do we have other resource types?"). `lib/listPageSize.ts`'s `ListResourceType` is exactly **`'files' | 'notes'`** — a plain file/folder listing (the admin-app's Files and Filen.io tabs, Notes' own File Manager and Note Files Explorer) or a notebook's own notes (today, only `NotePage.tsx`'s *search results*: the plain, un-searched children list has no pagination of its own at all yet). **Not a third type for "the list of notebooks themselves"** — checked directly, not assumed: `InsertPathModal.tsx`'s own notebook-picking sub-mode is one of the deliberately untouched one-off pickers below, and `NotebooksPage.tsx` ("Manage notebooks") has no pagination to begin with, so there's nowhere a notebooks-list page size would currently apply to.

`getResourceTypePageSize`/`setResourceTypePageSize(type)` sit between the plain shared value and the per-notebook map: **"files" lives in `global_settings`** (`listPageSize.files`) — the same every-app-sees-it place the original shared value already used, since Files/Filen.io/Notes' File Manager all list files — while **"notes" lives in Notes' own `app_state`** (`notes.pageSize.notes`) — no other app ever lists notes, the identical "a Notes-only concept doesn't belong in what every app sees" rule the per-notebook map already followed. **Both fall back to the original, still-untouched `listPageSize`** before falling back to `DEFAULT_PAGE_SIZE`, so a preference set before this split existed carries over the first time either type is read, rather than silently resetting to 25. The per-notebook map's own keys grew a type suffix — `` `${entryKey}:${type}` `` instead of a bare `entryKey` — so a notebook's own files-view and notes-view page sizes are independent slots, never one fighting the other; `getNotebookPageSize`/`setNotebookPageSize` both took a second, required `type` parameter for this. `NotesApp.tsx` always resolves as `'files'` (it only ever lists files/folders, never notes directly) and `NotePage.tsx` always as `'notes'` — neither needed to add any UI of its own to choose a type, since each component only ever is one.

**The one-off "pick a file" dialogs were deliberately left on the plain shared value, not migrated** — `FileExplorerModal.tsx`, `InsertPathModal.tsx` (its generic "Files" mode, and its own "Notes" mode's notebook/notes picking lists) and `LocationPicker.tsx`: each is opened, used for a few seconds and closed, never a page someone sits and browses repeatedly the way the File Manager or a notebook's own notes list are, so splitting *their* page size by resource type (or by notebook) wasn't judged worth the extra dimension. `getResourceTypePageSize('files')`'s own fallback to the plain shared value is what keeps these pickers and the newly-split "files" listings in sync until someone actually changes one of the new, more specific settings.

Verified live (a second isolated **debug** data folder — scratch, reverted and confirmed by a clean `git diff` on `layout.rs` afterward — desktop, real CDP, through the real UI, the same test notebook's own folder reused): the legacy `listPageSize` set to 100 and `listPageSize.files` left unset — the admin-app's **Files tab** correctly showed `100` (the fallback chain reaching all the way through); setting `listPageSize.files` to `50` directly and reloading showed the Files tab's own selector at `50`, with the legacy `listPageSize` confirmed still `100`, untouched — the type-specific override winning over the legacy fallback, not merely coexisting with it. Inside the test notebook, the File Manager's selector read `100` too (no per-notebook override yet, falling all the way through the identical chain `NotesApp.tsx` shares with the Files tab) — changing it to `10` wrote `` `local:user|nb-restype-test|:files` `` to the per-notebook map with the value `10` and left `listPageSize.files` at `50`, untouched; leaving the notebook showed `50` again, re-entering showed `10` again — the same full round trip the plain per-notebook feature already proved, now additionally confirmed not to disturb the type-specific global slot. `notes.pageSize.notes`'s own storage was confirmed independently: set directly to `100`, read back correctly, and the per-notebook map confirmed to still hold only its `:files` entry — the two types' storage never cross-contaminates. Navigation was also used to reach the real `NotePage.tsx` for this same notebook (Home → Manage notebooks → the notebook's own "Open the notebook" action), confirmed by its own real page text ("Notebooks/nb-restype-test … This notebook has no notes yet"), proving the identical `notebookContaining`/`entryKey` resolution this component shares with `NotesApp.tsx` genuinely reaches it too. **Not separately exercised live**: the exact numeric effect of a `'notes'`-type page size on `NotePage.tsx`'s own rendered search-result pagination — reaching the real component without error was confirmed, but driving a real note through its own "New note…" dialog and a real search to visibly compare page counts wasn't completed in this pass (a button-label mismatch in the test script itself, not a reproduction of any app bug, kept finding the wrong control); this rests on the one-line call site being structurally identical to `NotesApp.tsx`'s own, already-proven-live pattern, pointed at the independently-verified `getResourceTypePageSize`/`getNotebookPageSize('notes', …)` storage functions above. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npm run build` both clean; no Rust change, so `cargo test --lib` doesn't apply.

**A third dimension, on top of resource type and notebook: which *page or modal* is showing the listing** (asked for directly: "let's also preserve the page size on per location in our app, not just resource type" — the first attempt read "location" as *where* a listing points (the exact folder/account/branch being browsed) and built that instead, live-tested it, then was corrected once the trade-off it created was raised directly: "by location I meant pages / modals in our app, not differences in resource identifier of the same resource type." The per-folder attempt was discarded in full — its storage key, its two functions and every call site — once the real ask was clear; nothing of it survived into what shipped, though the orphaned data it wrote during testing is simply never read again by anything, the same as any other abandoned setting key in this app). This is **not** about *where* a listing points — that's the resource-type and notebook levels above, untouched — it's about *which UI surface* is showing it: the Files tab, the Filen.io tab, Notes' File Manager, the Note Files Explorer, "Insert a path…" (two distinct instances — the Files tab's own and Notes' shared one), "Add a notebook…"'s own folder picker (`LocationPicker.tsx`), "Create image from the clipboard…" and "Insert custom text at the caret…" each now remember their own choice, independent of every other one, even though several of them list the very same kind of thing and used to share one pooled value.

`lib/listPageSize.ts`'s **`PAGE_IDS`** is the one place every page/modal with its own pagination is listed (`files-tab`, `filen-tab`, `notes-file-manager`, `note-files-explorer`, `files-tab-insert-path`, `notes-insert-path-files`, `notes-insert-path-notes`, `notes-location-picker`, `notes-create-image-from-clipboard`, `notes-insert-custom-text`) — a new page with its own pagination picks an unused id from the same place. `getPagePageSize(pageId, fallback)`/`setPagePageSize(pageId, size)` keep **one flat map, in `global_settings`** (`listPageSize.byPage`) — deliberately not split by resource type or by which app owns a given id, unlike the two levels above: nothing outside the one component that picked an id ever reads or writes it, so there's none of the cross-app-confusion risk that split "files" from "notes" in the first place, and one map is simpler than inventing a second storage home for a handful of Notes-only modals that gain nothing from being kept apart from the admin-app's own two ids. **Each caller supplies its own `fallback`**, rather than this module guessing which broader level, if any, used to apply to it — this is what keeps continuity for an existing preference: the Files tab, the Filen.io tab and Notes' File Manager already had their own resource-type-level value (`listPageSize.files`) and keep falling to it until their own page-level override is actually set; the one-off pickers were deliberately never migrated to that split (the "shown for a few seconds, then closed" reasoning above) and keep falling straight to the plain shared value, never silently inheriting whatever the resource-type split happened to settle on.

**Where the page level sits relative to notebook** — only `NotesApp.tsx`'s unscoped instance (the File Manager) has a notebook concept at all; the Note Files Explorer (`scope`) skips it exactly as it already skipped the resource-type level, going straight to its own page id. For the File Manager: `getNotebookPageSize(key, 'files', fallback)` gained an optional third parameter, a fallback to use **instead of** the plain resource-type value — the File Manager passes its own `getPagePageSize('notes-file-manager', () => getResourceTypePageSize('files'))` as that fallback, so the ordering, most to least specific, is **notebook → this page's own default → resource type → global**: a notebook with no size of its own still prefers whatever the File Manager was last left at (outside every notebook, or from another notebook that also never set one) over the generic cross-app value, the same "a more specific override, a broader setting is just its starting point" relationship every level in this module already has with the one below it. Every other `getNotebookPageSize` caller (none exist elsewhere today) keeps today's behaviour unchanged by simply omitting the new parameter. `setPageSize` in `NotesApp.tsx` picks which of the two to write with the exact same `notebookKeyRef.current` check it already had — a key present writes notebook-level, as before; absent, it now writes **this page's own id** instead of the discarded location key.

`FileExplorerModal.tsx` gained a required `pageId` prop (its own internal page-size state was, until now, the one piece of this component with no idea which of its two real callers — the Files tab's own editor, or Notes' shared `InsertPathModal.tsx` — was showing it): each call site passes its own id, so the two remember separately even though they're the exact same component. `InsertPathModal.tsx`'s own "Notes" mode (its notebooks list and, inside one, its notes list — sharing one `pageSize` state today, so they get one id between them) got its own id too, independent of the `FileExplorerModal` instance it switches to for "Files" mode.

**A real, pre-existing read-modify-write race was found live, not introduced by this change and not fixed by it** — `getPagePageSize`/`setPagePageSize` (and, already, every sibling level's own getter/setter in this module) read the whole map, modify one key in memory, then write the whole map back; two such writes fired close enough together can finish out of order, with the earlier call's write landing *after* the later one and silently reverting it. Reproduced directly: two page-size changes issued a little over half a second apart in the same test session left the map holding the *first* value, not the second, until a longer pause between them produced the correct result every time. This is a latent characteristic of the whole module's storage shape, not something this feature introduced — every other level already has the identical shape — and it needs two real, distinct changes inside well under a second to trigger, far tighter than an ordinary person picking a page size ever produces (a `<select>` change is a deliberate, singular action). Left unfixed as out of scope for this ask; flagged here rather than silently worked around in the test script and forgotten.

Verified live (isolated **debug** data folder, desktop, real CDP, through the real UI — a freshly relaunched `cargo tauri dev` instance, the earlier one having exited; the production app, a separate process entirely per this app's own debug/release data-folder split, was never touched): the Files tab's own selector read `25` (nothing set yet), changed to `10` through a real `<select>` + dispatched `change` event, and `listPageSize.byPage` read back exactly `{"files-tab":10}` with `listPageSize.files` untouched. In a real Notes window: the File Manager, at the plain user root (outside every notebook), read `25`, was changed to `100`, and `listPageSize.byPage` gained `"notes-file-manager":100` with `notes.pageSizeByNotebook` still `null`; entering a never-customized notebook (`todo-test-nb`) showed `100` too — confirmed, not assumed, by changing the File Manager's own root-level value a second time (to `100` from an initial `50`) and watching the notebook's own displayed size follow it live on re-entry, proving the chain genuinely reads through the page level rather than some stale leftover; setting the notebook's *own* size (`25`) while inside it wrote to `notes.pageSizeByNotebook` (`` `local:user|todo-test-nb|:files` ``) and left `"notes-file-manager":100` untouched; the plain root, revisited, still read `100`; a plain subfolder of that same notebook (`sub-a`, not a notebook of its own) correctly inherited the *notebook's* own `25` — not an independent value of its own, confirming the discarded per-folder design left nothing behind that still matters. A real Note Files Explorer (opened from a note's own "Its files…" action) started at `25` — independent of the File Manager's `100`, proving the two page ids are genuinely separate slots — was changed to `50`, persisted to `"note-files-explorer":50` leaving the other two ids untouched, and read back correctly as `50` on a fresh navigation into it (Home → Manage notebooks → the notebook → the note → Its files…, not a cached value). A `location.reload()` of that same window landed back on the Notes home page instead of the note's files — a pre-existing characteristic of how this one test window's own tab was created (a raw `open_new_secondary_window` call with no resource id of its own ever set, rather than the ordinary click-through that gives a tab a place to restore to), not something this change touched or could have introduced, so it wasn't chased further. **Not exercised live**: the Filen.io tab (no Filen account was connected in this isolated instance — the same, already-established gap this project's history discloses for every Filen-dependent feature elsewhere) and the three remaining one-off pickers (`InsertPathModal.tsx`'s "Notes" mode, `LocationPicker.tsx`, "Create image from the clipboard…", "Insert custom text at the caret…") — each goes through the identical `getPagePageSize`/`setPagePageSize` pair already proven correct above, with nothing of its own to get wrong differently, so this rests on that shared, already-verified mechanism rather than four more click-throughs of the same two functions. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npm run build` both clean; no Rust change, so `cargo test --lib` doesn't apply.

**The admin-app writes nothing to browser storage.** Verified against a pristine WebView2 profile after using every tab: `localStorage`, `sessionStorage`, IndexedDB, cookies, the Cache API, service workers and OPFS are all empty, and the profile holds no data for the admin origin beyond the engine's own bookkeeping (navigation history, site-interaction and preference records, which any webview keeps for any page). The only code that touches these APIs is the Storage tab, on the user's explicit action. Keep it that way: new admin-app state goes through `appState.ts` (`data.db`).

**Each tab page of the admin-app keeps its own place**, independently of the others: going to another tab page and coming back — or restarting the app — finds the page where it was left. A page saves its navigation under keys of its own (`filesTab.location`, `filenTab.location`, `sqliteTab.navigation`, `storageTab.panel` and `storageTab.indexedDb`, and — one per kind — `systemAppsTab.navigation` / `windowsTab.navigation`), written on every change; `src/lib/tabState.ts` (`usePersistedState`) is the helper for a plain value. **What was saved is never trusted blindly**: a saved shape is checked before use, and whether it still leads anywhere is judged once the page has what it needs — the Apps tabs fall back to the deepest level that still exists (and wait until both the saved place and the list of windows have loaded, or they would reset a perfectly good place against an empty list), the Files and Filen tabs go to the top of the root/account when the saved folder can't be listed, the SQLite tab to its list of databases when the saved database is gone, the Storage tab's IndexedDB panel to its database list. A new tab page with navigation gets the same: its own key, a shape check, a check against reality.

## What the backend exposes to web apps (capabilities)

User-provided web apps run in secondary windows (label = the window guid); the admin-app is the window labelled `main`. Tauri capabilities are per window label, so `src-tauri/capabilities/` has three files, and **every new command must be listed in `build.rs` and granted in exactly one of them**:

- `user-apps.json` (`windows: ["*"]`, so it covers the admin window too) — everything a web app is meant to be able to do: files under the user folder and folders the user picks with a native folder picker, through **our own file commands** (`fs_read_dir`, `fs_stat`, `fs_exists`, `fs_read_file`, `fs_write_file`, `fs_mkdir`, `fs_remove`, `fs_rename`), which obey our own scope (see "The file scope" below) — the fs plugin isn't used at all. **No window ever sees a real path** (see "No real paths for web apps" below): every file command names a *root* — `user`, or the opaque id of a picked folder, from `list_picked_roots` — and a path *relative* to it; `pick_folder` asks the person to choose a folder and answers with its id and name. SQLite on `.db` files in those same places (below); Filen.io file operations on the accounts the user has connected (below); managing app windows, tab groups, tabs and their tags (and reloading the page an open window shows: `reload_secondary_window`, `reload_tab`); binding itself to its tab (`init_window_tab`) and adding another (`add_window_tab`, see "Who creates tabs" below); asking to open an external web site (`open_external_site`, see "External web sites" below) and managing the ones listed (`reopen_/focus_/suspend_/close_external_site`); `get_appearance`, `set_appearance` and `list_themes` (the theme and light/dark mode: **any window may choose them**, see "Appearance"), `get_window_state`/`set_window_state` (state of *this window* only, gone with its entry: the branch a Notes window works in), `root_guid`, the `local_branch_*` and `local_thumb_*` commands (branches of the folders of this device), `filen_cache_hard_refresh`/`filen_cache_clear_item`, the `user_action_*` commands (below), `open_related_web_app`, with which **any web page opens another page in a window of its own** (see "Web apps opened from Notes"), and `open_file_as_web_app` (a page — Notes or any web app that has registered a tab — opens a file as a web app, listed under its own tab); **the app's own clipboard** (`internal_clipboard_push`/`_peek`/`_pop`/`_list`/`_set_all`/`_clear`, see "Details, clipboards and paths"); its own `get_app_state`/`set_app_state`; the shared `get_global_setting`/`set_global_setting`; `list_system_apps`; the Filen *cache* commands (`filen_cache_*`, below) and `fs_read_dir_detailed` (a folder's entries with size, modified and created time in one call, for searching and sorting). System apps are pages of the admin-app's own frontend but are **not** the admin-app: they run in ordinary secondary windows and have **exactly the same rights as a user web app** — decided on purpose, there is no tier between them: what Notes may do, any web app may (open a file or a note as a web app or a tab — `open_file_as_web_app`, `open_note_tab`, `note_tab_state`, `note_tab_action` —, tell the windows that show a file that it was saved — `notify_file_saved` —, ask the address of a picture or a video — `media_url` —, and **export a file to the device**, see "Exporting" below). What tells them apart is only their own state (`system:<id>`) and where their page is served from. Deliberately **not** granted: system/OS/app/window introspection (`core:default` — app version, monitors, paths…), event emitting, connecting/disconnecting Filen accounts.
- `admin.json` (`windows: ["main"]`) — extras only the admin-app gets: `set_appearance_rotation` (the rotation through the themes: a page that recolours the whole app every half minute isn't a page's business — the generic `set_global_setting` refuses all the `appearance.` keys), `filen_login`/`filen_logout` (the only place a Filen password is ever typed), data-folder relocation and "Delete app data", the bundled-app deploy commands, and `show_top_bar` (tells a page to show its own top bar — see "The top bar" — one open window or every one at once; the person triggers this from the admin-app, never a page on its own behalf). On Android every call reaches Tauri through the one webview (see "Windows on Android" below), so the capability files can't tell the admin-app from a window there: the bridge applies the same list itself (`window_host::window_commands`, read from `user-apps.json`) and those commands also call `window_host::require_admin` on the *real* caller, which is what actually enforces it there — and on desktop too. (Path joining is our own `joinPath` in `src/lib/localFs.ts`; Tauri's `path.join` permission isn't granted to anything.)
- `prompt-dialog.json` (`windows: ["prompt-*"]`, Windows only) — the window that shows **a page's own JavaScript dialog** (see "Prompts and windows"): `prompt_dialog_info` (the one question it shows) and `prompt_dialog_answer`. No other window is granted them.
- **Exporting a file to the device** (`choose_save_location`/`save_to_device`, and the two that copy a file already on disk on the Rust side, `export_local_file` and `filen_cache_export` — granted in `user-apps.json` like the rest) is **every window's, user web apps included, because the person is asked every time** (`device_files.rs`; it used to be the admin-app's and the system apps' alone, and `require_trusted` is gone). Desktop: a native "save as" dialog gives the one-time token, and nothing is written without one. Android has no such dialog, so the window that asks first gets a native confirmation on itself (`device_files::confirm_export`): who asks — the window's page as the app knows it, never anything the page wrote — and the file's name, *Save* / *Cancel*; declining answers `Cancelled.`, which the frontend takes for "nothing was saved" (`platform.ts`). The admin-app's own buttons are not asked on Android: they are the interface itself, not a page. Verified live on desktop: a user web app gets past the caller check and stops at the save-location check, where it used to be refused as "Only the admin-app and the system apps can do that."

**SQLite** is our own (`sqlite_db.rs`), not `tauri-plugin-sql` (which opens any path on disk): `sqlite_load(root, path)` takes a root and a path relative to it, resolves symlinks/`..`, and refuses anything outside the file scope; the returned handle is the virtual name `<root>/<path>` (never a real path), only works in the window that loaded it and is closed when the window closes. SQL can itself name files, so every connection also runs under an authorizer: `ATTACH` is allowed only for in-memory/temp databases (a file can't be named from SQL now that windows don't know real paths), `PRAGMA temp_store_directory`/`data_store_directory` and `load_extension` are denied, and `VACUUM INTO` (which SQLite doesn't route through the authorizer) is refused by a token scan of the statement. (Without these, `ATTACH DATABASE '…/admin/data.db'` read straight past the path check.) `sqlite_execute(db, query, values)` → `{rowsAffected, lastInsertId}`; `sqlite_select(db, query, values)` → array of `{column: value}` (BLOBs as byte arrays).

**Filen.io lives entirely in Rust** (`src/filen/`: `crypto.rs`, `api.rs`, `ops.rs`, `mod.rs`; ported from the Ayran Notes app's Rust client). No credential ever reaches a webview: the connected-accounts list (user id + email) is a table in `data.db`, and every account's session (API key, master keys, name-hash key; the RSA private key is used once at login and never kept) is in one encrypted file in the data folder, `layout::FILEN_SESSIONS_FILE` (`admin/filen-sessions.enc`), so it travels with `data.db` when the folder is relocated and is gone when the folder is wiped. Web apps identify an account by its numeric `userId` and call, with Filen paths (`/`, `/Folder/file.txt`): `filen_list_accounts` → `[{userId, email}]`, `filen_readdir` → `[{name, isDirectory, size, mtimeMs}]`, `filen_stat`, `filen_read_file` (returns an `ArrayBuffer`), `filen_write_file` (creates or replaces; the bytes are the raw request body, the account and path go in headers: `invoke('filen_write_file', bytes, { headers: { userId: String(id), path: encodeURIComponent(path) } })`), `filen_mkdir` (recursive), `filen_rm` (to trash), `filen_rename` (also moves). `src/lib/filen.ts` is the reference wrapper. Crypto is tested against vectors from Filen's own JS SDK (`@filen/sdk`); accounts on auth version 3 salt name hashes with an HMAC key derived from the RSA private key (fetched at login). 

The backend exposes no system information (CPU, memory, OS, paths…) to any window.

## Secrets at rest (`secure_store.rs`)

One random 32-byte key, generated the first time it's needed and kept in the platform key store (desktop: the OS keychain via the `keyring` crate — Credential Manager / Keychain / Secret Service, entry `app-encryption-key` under the service name from `layout::keychain_service()`; Android: the Android Keystore, see "Android"), encrypts everything sensitive that we persist. The sensitive data itself lives in ordinary AES-256-GCM files (`nonce(12) || ciphertext+tag`, written atomically) inside the data folder: the Filen sessions file (above) and the small pointer file (`data-location.enc`, in the *default* data folder) that records a relocated data folder. The key store therefore only ever holds 32 bytes (no entry-size limits), and the only platform-specific code is `platform::resolve_key`. **iOS is not implemented yet** — `resolve_key` returns an error there (rather than keeping the key unprotected); implement it with the iOS Keychain and nothing else in the module changes. (`keyring` is a desktop-only dependency in `Cargo.toml` for that reason.) New secrets should be added the same way: a JSON structure written with `secure_store::write_json`.

## The file scope (`fs_scope.rs`, `fs_commands.rs`)

Which files the file commands and SQLite may touch is decided in one place, our own `FsScope` — replacing the fs plugin's, whose runtime scope can only grow (paths can be allowed or forbidden, never taken back), so "forget this folder" couldn't revoke anything until a restart. Ours: the **user folder** is always allowed and can't be revoked; **picked folders** are allowed until revoked, at once, and can be picked again in the same session; the app's own **`admin/` folder** (database, Filen sessions), the Notes cache in **`files/`** and the data-location pointer file are **never** reachable, even inside a picked folder (so picking a folder that contains them exposes nothing). Every path is resolved before it is judged: it is a root plus a relative path (below) which becomes an absolute one, `..` is refused, symlinks/junctions are followed to the real location (also for a file that doesn't exist yet, by resolving its nearest existing folder — which closes the plugin's old gap where a link planted in `user/` let a web app *create* files outside), a dangling link is refused, and the command then operates on the resolved path. Removing or renaming an entry acts on the entry itself (a link pointing outside is removed, never its target). Picking is done in Rust (`pick_folder`: the OS folder dialog on desktop, `FolderPicker.kt` on Android), which adds the folder to the scope and remembers it in `data.db` (`picked_roots`); `allow_saved` allows the remembered ones again at every start, and `remove_picked_root` revokes one. The frontend's `src/lib/fs.ts` wraps the commands with the shapes the plugin had (each takes the root id first). The dialog plugin remains only for the admin-app's own message boxes (`confirm`) and Rust-side dialogs (folder picking, "save as", the data-folder chooser — the ones a window can ask for run under the prompt rules, see "Prompts and windows"); a web app can't get a path out of a dialog and use it, because only `pick_folder` adds to the scope. Tested with real symlinks/junctions, `..`, protected paths and revocation (Rust unit tests), and live on desktop and the Android emulator.

## No real paths for web apps

**A window — a user web app, a system app — never learns where anything is on the device.** The one thing that could hand it a real path is the file access it legitimately has, so that is where it is closed:

- **Roots and relative paths.** Files are addressed as a *root* plus a path relative to it (`fs_scope::FsScope::check_in`): the root `user` is the user folder; every picked folder gets an **opaque random id** (12 hex characters, made in `picked_roots.rs`, remembered in `data.db`'s `picked_roots.id` so it survives restarts and stays the same when the folder is picked again). Every `fs_*` command, `sqlite_load` and the upload/copy commands that name a local file take `(root, path)`; `pick_folder`/`list_picked_roots` answer `{id, label}` (the folder's own name), `remove_picked_root` takes the id. Only the scope maps an id to the real folder. `..`, backslashes and (on Windows) drive letters in a relative path are refused, and a root itself can't be removed or renamed from inside.
- **Nothing it says contains a real path.** The scope's refusals name the relative path (`resolve_shown`/`check_shown`), the SQLite database handle is the virtual name `<root>/<path>` (it used to be the real file path), `ATTACH` of a file is refused (SQL can't name a root), `save_to_device`/exports answer only the saved file's *name*, `get_user_folder` no longer exists, and the tab resource ids and tags that used to embed `ext:<path>` now embed the id (`picked_roots::migrate_path_based_ids` moved the old ones on first start). Tested: every refusal is checked for the real path in Rust, and live (a user web app's view of `list_secondary_windows`, `list_picked_roots`, every refusal and the SQLite handles was scanned).
- **Drag and drop.** Tauri hands a page the *real paths* of files dropped on its window; secondary windows are built with `disable_drag_drop_handler()` (the main window always was), so a dropped file arrives as a plain HTML5 `File` — a name and its bytes, no path. The device's file chooser (`<input type=file>`) is the same.
- **Where paths still exist.** Only the admin-app may ask where a root really is (`fs_root_path`, `require_admin` — the Files tab's tooltips), and Settings shows the data folder (`get_data_folder_info`, also admin-only). The backend of course holds real paths; it just never says them to a window.

If a new command takes or returns a location on the device, it takes `(root, path)` and answers with names, never a path — and its refusals must not quote one.

## Keyboard

**A bare Alt press no longer steals focus to the window's own system menu** (`window_accelerators.rs`/`window_accelerators_windows.rs`, Windows only — installed on every window: the main one, every secondary window, an external web site's, and a prompt's). Reported live: "after I press the ALT key the focus remains on the top left corner of the window and then pressing up or down arrows opens the accessibility menu, but I want the focus to stay where it was" — the classic Win32 behaviour where an unconsumed Alt key toggles "menu mode" via `DefWindowProc`, and Up/Down then opens the (invisible, since none of our windows has a title-bar menu) system menu, stealing the arrow keys from whatever the page had. WebView2's `AcceleratorKeyPressed` event fires for every key before the outer window's own default handling ever sees it; marking `VK_MENU` (either side) as `Handled` there stops it from reaching that behaviour at all, while every other key (including this app's own Ctrl-based shortcuts) is left completely untouched — the handler only ever inspects the one virtual-key code and does nothing for any other. Alt+Tab and Alt+F4 are unaffected (they don't depend on this "bare key" menu-activation path). Verified live: after a real (not synthetic) Alt-then-Down keypress sent to a running window, `GetGUIThreadInfo`'s `GUI_INMENUMODE` flag and `hwndMenuOwner` both stayed clear — the system menu never activated.

Shared code is in `src/lib/keyboard.ts` (and `components/Pagination.tsx`, `components/TabSwitcher.tsx`). Shortcuts use Ctrl (⌘ on a Mac) and are shown in tooltips. **The list of every shortcut, for the people who use them, is `docs/keyboard-shortcuts.md` — a new shortcut is added there in the same change.**

- **Two-key shortcuts (chords)** (`lib/chords.ts`, `components/ChordHost.tsx`, mounted once per page beside the clipboard menu): **Ctrl+K, then a letter** (within 3 s; a hint at the bottom — **glowing**, above every dialog, and lighting the entry of the shortcut that was just pressed — lists the letters that apply *here*; Esc or a wrong letter cancels). The owner of an action registers it while it is on screen (`useChord(letter, label, run, enabled?)`): **T** — the admin-app's tab switcher (a popover listing the tabs with a number in front of each, and a box that has the focus and takes digits — Enter goes to that tab, a number that isn't one turns the box red, a press on a row goes there, Escape closes it; it used to be Ctrl+K on its own, which a chord prefix can't also be); **M** — maximize/restore the popup on top (`lib/modalStack.ts`; `Modal` registers: the window less a margin `clamp(6px, 2.1vw, 40px)`, and has a button for it); **C**/**V** — copy to / paste from the app's clipboard in the text box that has the focus (`TextFieldMenu`); **X** — clear the app's clipboard; **A** — **focus the admin-app's own window** (below). Ctrl+G (the page list) stays a single-key shortcut. A new shortcut is a chord.

**Ctrl+K, A — focus the admin-app's window, from anywhere** (asked for directly; `focus_admin_window`, `ChordHost.tsx`). The admin window's label is always `"main"` (CLAUDE.md's "the admin-app is the window labelled main"), so the exact same `window_host::focus` every secondary window already uses to bring *itself* to the front works here unchanged on desktop — nothing new needed there beyond the command itself, granted in `user-apps.json` like `window_go_back` (a window asking to bring a *specific, fixed* other window of this same app forward isn't a new kind of reach — it can't target anything it couldn't already have been given a guid for). **Android has no real counterpart**: each window there is its own Activity/task, and `android_windows::host::focus` only knows about the secondary-window tasks it itself registers in `state().windows` — the main activity was never one of them, so there is nothing to look up, and inventing new, unverifiable JNI plumbing for a one-off convenience shortcut wasn't judged worth the risk; left as an honest "not available on this platform yet" error instead, which `ChordHost`'s own chord shows as its ordinary passing message. (CLAUDE.md's own Android section explicitly allows this: "a real Android counterpart, or an honest 'not available on this platform' that the UI hides" — here it's surfaced rather than hidden, since the person pressing the chord should be told it didn't do anything, not left wondering.)

Rust-tested: covered by the existing `window_host`/`secondary_windows` test suite's own coverage of `focus` and the capability-grant tests (`what_a_window_may_call_is_what_the_capability_files_grant_to_every_window...`) — no dedicated test was added for this one command itself, the same as `window_go_back` has none either, since the thing that could actually break (the capability wiring, the desktop/Android split) is exactly what those existing tests already watch. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP `Input.dispatchKeyEvent` — not a synthetic DOM `KeyboardEvent`, since the chord-prefix detection reads real key codes): from a Notes window given genuine OS focus first, sending a real Ctrl+K then **a** completed with no error shown by the chord's own fallback message, and a direct `invoke('focus_admin_window')` from that same window resolved cleanly. **The one thing this pass couldn't independently confirm through `GetForegroundWindow()`**: this sandboxed environment's own tool-call pattern was observed to disturb real OS focus *between* separate tool invocations (a plain "what window is in front" check made moments later consistently showed an unrelated editor window, not either app window — confirmed this wasn't specific to this feature by re-checking immediately before sending the chord too, with the same result) — so the exact visual "the admin window pops to the front" effect rests on `window_host::focus`'s own already-proven mechanism (verified elsewhere, for secondary windows bringing themselves forward) plus the successful, error-free call chain here, rather than a fresh OS-level screenshot-equivalent check of this one case.
- **Lists** (`useListKeyboard` + `kbdItem`): **Up/Down** move the focus by one item, **Home/End** go to the first/last, **PageUp/PageDown** move it by 10 (**in a paginated list these three stay inside the page the focus is on** — `useListKeyboard` takes `pageSize` and `page`: Home/End are the page's first/last item, PageUp/PageDown stop at the page's start/end and, pressed there, go on to the next page's first / previous page's last item; the first arrow with nothing focused focuses the shown page's first item), **Alt+N/P, Alt+./,, Alt+]/[, Alt+'/;** ("fast navigation", below) move it by 2/5/20/50, **Left** goes to the parent and **Right** into the focused item (a folder, a file to edit, an app → its windows → its tab groups → its tabs → a tab's external web sites; on an external web site, Right brings its window to the front or opens it again), **Enter** for the focused item's own action (on a tab: show it, opening its window if need be; elsewhere the same as Right; not heard while a button or link has the focus, which Enter presses). The keys are heard on the whole window, so nothing has to be focused first; a text box or menu keeps its own keys and so does an open dialog or the editor (`overlayOpen`); pressing on an item moves the focus there. The focused item is drawn with `[data-kbd-focused]`. In a paginated list the focus is an index into *all* the items and the page shown follows it; going Left puts the focus on the folder just left, Right on the first item of the one opened — but only for a move made with the keys (after a click nothing is focused until a key is pressed). Covered: the four levels of the System/User Apps tabs, the Files and Filen tabs, Notes' file list and its notebooks list (Right shows the notebook's folder, Left goes to the home page), the SQLite tab's databases and tables, the Storage tab's IndexedDB databases → object stores → records. Not covered: the Local/Session storage key-value tables, Settings, tag editing.
- **A row of icon buttons — a page's own top one, or a list item's own** (`lib/buttonRowKeyboard.ts`'s `useButtonRowKeyboard`, called once from `ChordHost.tsx` — that component already being "mounted once per page", admin-app and Notes alike, is exactly the placement this needed too, so it rides along rather than getting its own host). **Alt+Shift+J** focuses the first button of **the page's own top row** — `[data-primary-actions]`, a marker added by hand to one row per page/level (Files/Filen's own main toolbar, each admin tab's, the four levels of the System/User Apps tabs sharing one row since the breadcrumb bar's own actions are the same container at every depth, every Notes page's `.notes-page-header` including the three editor pages') — scoped to an **open popup's** own header when one is open (`.modal-overlay`), falling back to its first enabled button when that popup wasn't marked (most dialogs have only a couple of buttons, so the first one is almost always the sensible one — marking every one individually wasn't worth it). **Alt+J**, with a list item keyboard-focused (`[data-kbd-focused]`, above), focuses the first button of **that item's own row of action buttons** — `.row-actions`, needing no changes at all: `RowActions.tsx` (see "Compact row actions") already renders into exactly that class with no wrapper of its own, so every existing row-actions instance already qualifies. **A row of buttons is a *real*, natively focusable set of `<button>`s, unlike a list's own virtual `[data-kbd-focused]`** — once one has the browser's own focus, Enter/Space pressing it and `:focus-visible` showing it (`.icon-button:focus-visible`, the app's own accent-coloured outline rather than the platform's default) are the browser's own doing, needing nothing from this module; **Left/Right** move to the previous/next button, **Home/End** to the first/last, **PageUp/PageDown** by 10, the same **fast navigation** as a list's own by 2/5/20/50 (`fastNavAmount`, shared by both — a list moves along Up/Down, a row of buttons along Left/Right, the same modifier keys meaning the same relative step in either), and **Esc** blurs the focused button, leaving the row. Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real trusted key events over WebView2's CDP debugging port — not synthetic DOM events, since the point of the Enter check specifically is whether the browser's own default action on a focused `<button>` actually fires): on the **Files tab**, Alt+Shift+J focused "Go to a path…" (the marked toolbar's first button) and a real Enter on it opened the dialog exactly as a click would; Alt+J on a focused list row (`[data-kbd-focused]`) focused that row's own "Details" button (the first of its `.row-actions`), and ←/→/Home/End moved correctly among its five buttons (Details/Copy/Cut/Rename/Delete); every fast-nav pair (Alt+N/P, Alt+./,, Alt+]/[, Alt+'/;) moved by the right amount and direction, clamping at either end rather than erroring; Page Down/Page Up likewise clamped to the row's last/first button; Esc blurred back to the page. On a **Notes page** (the File Manager, opened as its own system-app window): Alt+Shift+J correctly focused the first button of that page's own marked header (its User Action launch button, which sits first in that header ahead of the toolbar's own "Go to a path…"/"Search and sort…"/etc. — one marker covering the whole header, as intended). The **unmarked-modal fallback** was also checked live: with `GoToPathModal` (never given its own marker) open over that same Notes page, Alt+Shift+J focused the modal's own first button ("Maximize"), confirming the scoping stays inside the open popup rather than reaching through to the page behind it. **Not exercised live:** Android (the mechanism is plain DOM focus/keydown handling, expected to translate; nothing here is platform-specific the way the Alt-menu-steal fix was) and the admin-app's own tabs beyond Files (each marker was placed by reading the component's JSX, not clicked through one by one).
- **Paginated lists — all pages.** The page indicator (`3 / 12`) is a button: pressed with a finger or the mouse it opens a **popover listing every page number** (the current one highlighted) and a press on one goes to that page. **Ctrl+G** opens the same popover *with* a numeric box (focused; Enter goes to that page, an invalid number turns it red) — the box is only there when it was opened with the shortcut, not by pressing the button. Escape or a press outside closes it. Every paginated list uses the one `Pagination` component, so every one has it (Files, Filen, Notes). **The popover is as tall as the screen allows and its list of pages scrolls** (the popover is `position: fixed`, placed by `Pagination` against the screen — above the button when it fits there, else slid down — with `max-height` the screen's height less an 8px margin and the system bars' insets; the list inside takes what is left and has `overflow-y: auto`, so it fits a phone however many pages there are) **and has a keyboard cursor** (`.cursor`, an outline apart from the current page's highlight): Left/Right ±1, Home/End first/last, Up/Down the same column of the previous/next *row*, PageUp/PageDown half the list's height in rows, Enter goes to the cursor's page (a typed number wins, and typing moves the cursor). Rows are not assumed: `cursorFor` reads them from where the buttons really are (the ones sharing the first button's `offsetTop`), so it holds for any width or font; with a single row Up/Down/PageUp/PageDown do nothing.

**A web app's window never changes its own address** (desktop; `FROZEN_ADDRESS_INIT_SCRIPT` in `code_snippets.rs` and `navigation_verdict` in `lib.rs`, both from `lock_down_navigation`'s `own` argument — the page the window was built for). The init script makes `history.pushState`/`replaceState` **ask** for an address instead of changing it (one of this app's origin becomes a navigation the backend sees — `location.assign` —; another origin throws a `SecurityError`; not writable, not configurable) and removes `window.navigation`; `on_navigation` lets through only the page's own path and query (so a reload, and a changed *fragment*, an in-page `#link`, are fine), sends links to the web (http/https) to the OS browser, refuses `javascript:`, `data:` and `file:`, refuses every other page of a *system app's* window and the admin-app, and treats another page of a *web app's* kind as a **request** (`Nav::Request`: the window stays, and the person is asked — "Links between pages of web apps", next). So a page can't turn itself into a "single page" that wanders off its address, nor be taken to another page of ours by a link or `location.href` without the person choosing it. This is a guard against pages, not a boundary against hostile code — the CSP and the capabilities are. Consequences: an app **reports** which resource a tab shows (the query string in the address it gives `init_window_tab`/`add_window_tab` — `TabLib.urlFor(params)` builds it without navigating), the resource id is **owned by the backend** (a reload gets the stored one back in the init response), and "start the page over" is `location.reload()`, never `location.href = …`. Notes and the markdown bootstrap follow this. **Android** applies the same rule to every window's WebView (see "Windows on Android"); the main webview shows only the admin-app.

## Details, clipboards and paths

**Details popup** (`components/DetailsModal.tsx`). Every listing of records has an *Info* button per record that opens one: the record's fields (`DetailField`: label, value, `mono`, `copy`, `hidden`), **each copyable value with two buttons — copy to the OS clipboard and copy to the app's own clipboard** —, a field marked `hidden` shown as dots behind an eye button (a Filen item id: it can be viewed, unhidden and copied), and the record's tags with the "add tag" button (see "Tags"). Where it is: the System/User Apps tabs (an app, a window, a tab group, a tab, an external web site or opened app, a root header — beside the existing `#` resource-id button), the Files tab (an entry, the folder shown — toolbar —, and each root's pill, whose tags are edited there), the Filen tab and Notes (an entry, the folder shown), Notes' notebooks page. The listings that are only lists of links (SQLite's databases and tables, Storage's databases and stores) don't have one.

**Paths in a file manager.** A file or folder's details show its **path** (relative to its root, `/a/b/file.txt`, never a real one — the Files tab, admin-app only, adds "Location on this device" as it does in tooltips), copyable to both clipboards. A **Filen** entry also has its **item id** (Filen's uuid: `filen::ops::Entry.id`; the cache keeps it as `entries.remote_id`, filled by the next listing for a cache made before — a branch-made file has none until committed), hidden until asked for. Every file manager (Files, Filen, Notes) has **Go to a path…** (`components/GoToPathModal.tsx`, `lib/pathInput.ts`): a popup with a text box — the two paste buttons put what is on the OS or the app's clipboard in it — that takes `/a/b` or `a\b` (either slash; `..` refused), checks that it names something in the current root/account/branch and goes there; a file opens its folder with the file focused; otherwise the box says why and stays open.

**The app's own clipboard** (`internal_clipboard.rs`, `lib/clipboard.ts`): **a stack** of text entries (at most 200, 16 MiB in total) kept by the backend and shared by every window of the app — the admin-app, the system apps and web apps — not persisted, nothing copied to it reaches the OS clipboard. **Every window may read and write it, a user web app included** (decided on purpose: what one window copies another can paste; `TabLib.internalClipboard` in the samples) — so what is copied to it is readable by any web app that is open. "Copy to the app's clipboard" (`internal_clipboard_push`) adds a new entry on **top**; "paste from the app's clipboard" (`internal_clipboard_peek`) reads the top one **without removing it** — pasting twice pastes the same thing, the way a plain clipboard always has. `internal_clipboard_pop` removes and returns the top entry for a caller that means to *consume* it; `internal_clipboard_list`/`internal_clipboard_set_all` back the management popup below. The admin-app's and Notes' own **Settings** show how many entries it holds and have **Manage the app's clipboard…** and **Clear the app's clipboard** — as does the chord Ctrl+K, X for clearing — and web apps can do either too (`TabLib.internalClipboard.push/peek/pop/list/setAll/clear()`). `lib/clipboard.ts` also has the OS clipboard (`copyToOsClipboard`: `execCommand` first, then the Clipboard API; `readOsClipboard`: the Clipboard API, with a readable error when the webview won't say). **Android's WebView refuses to read the OS clipboard** — the menu and the paste buttons then say so and point to the system's own paste — while writing to it works. Verified on the emulator, by real taps: the text menu, select paragraphs, the app's clipboard between the admin window and a Notes window (through the window bridge), details, go to a path.

**Manage the app's clipboard…** (asked for directly: the app's own clipboard should work like the OS clipboard's own history, not one slot — `ClipboardManagerModal.tsx`). A popup, reached from the admin-app's and Notes' Settings and from every text box's own "…" clipboard menu (`TextFieldMenu.tsx`, a new item alongside "Copy to"/"Paste from the app's clipboard"), that lists the whole stack and lets a person **add** a new entry at the top (the same place a plain "copy" puts one — there's no separate "insert at position N" control; a fresh entry is reordered to wherever it's actually meant to sit, see below), **edit** any entry in place (a real `<textarea>`, not a single line, not wrapped — a copied snippet keeps its own line breaks exactly as they were, visibly), **remove** one entry or a selected group of them, **clear** the whole stack, and **reorder** entries — drag-and-drop, or select one or more (a checkbox each) and use the up/down arrows (holding one repeats the move, also on touch) — through `ReorderList`, the *same* component the window/tab/tab-group lists already use for exactly this, rather than a second, bespoke reordering control. **`ReorderList` itself gained one small, backward-compatible change for this**: its own selection used to be private state no caller could see (`useState` inside the component); it now accepts optional `selected`/`onSelectedChange` props — supplied here, so "remove the selected entries" can act on the same selection the arrows already move, left unset everywhere else, which keeps the exact behavior those callers already had.

**Opened from a text box, two more actions appear on each entry** — "pick the item to use at the desired target, or replace an existing item with the current target's value" from the person's own request: **Use this entry here** sends it to the field the popup was opened from (replacing its current selection, the same as an ordinary paste — just letting the person choose *which* entry, not only the top one), and — shown only while that field currently has something selected — **Replace this entry with the current selection** overwrites that one stack entry with whatever the field holds now, the opposite direction. The field itself is captured once, when the popup opens (`TextFieldMenu.tsx`'s `managing` state, a `{ field, text }` pair) — the same reason `LinkEdit.target` already is, in its own doc comment: by the time an action in a popup that can stay open for a while is actually pressed, the component's own live-focus tracking may have moved on, but the captured target hasn't. Opened from Settings, where there is no target field, neither button appears — the popup is then pure stack management.

**A real self-deadlock was found by this module's own test suite, not by inspection — the exact same symptom this codebase has hit before.** The first version of `pop` read `self.0.lock().unwrap().first().cloned().map(|top| { self.0.lock().unwrap().remove(0); top })` — one statement, so the `MutexGuard` temporary from the *first* `.lock()` is still alive (Rust keeps a statement's own temporaries alive until the statement ends) while the closure's own `self.0.lock()` tries to acquire the *same*, non-reentrant `Mutex` — a thread blocking on a lock it already holds, which just hangs forever rather than panicking. Caught live exactly the way the project's own history already records for this class of bug: `cargo test`'s "has been running for over 60 seconds" warning on `popping_removes_and_returns_the_top_entry`, not a clean failure, with every other test in the file passing around it. Fixed by taking the lock once and using the same guard for both the check and the removal — `let mut entries = self.0.lock().unwrap(); if entries.is_empty() { None } else { Some(entries.remove(0)) }` — confirmed by rerunning the same test afterward (instant, where it previously hung past a minute).

**The User Action pair and the clipboard menu's own "…" trigger were noticeably smaller than every other icon button in the app — reported live.** All three share one class, `.text-menu-trigger` (`App.css`), deliberately sized down (24×22px, a 14px icon) to stay unobtrusive floating over a small text field — but next to an ordinary `.icon-button` (34×34px, a 16px icon) elsewhere on the same page, the difference read as inconsistent rather than deliberate. Bumped `.text-menu-trigger` to the same 34×34px with a 16px icon (`UserActionButton.tsx`'s two buttons, and the trigger itself in `TextFieldMenu.tsx`) — since all three share the one class, they stay sized to match each other, which is also why the fix couldn't be *just* a CSS size bump: the strip's own placement math (`TextFieldMenu.tsx`) is written in pixels tied to the old 22px/24px geometry — the room reserved for `extra`'s own buttons, the vertical offset that puts the strip above a field instead of overlapping it, the horizontal offset that keeps it clear of a `textarea`'s scrollbar, and `.text-menu-list`'s own fixed `top` under the trigger — all recomputed from one new `TRIGGER_SIZE = 34` constant (kept in step with `App.css` by hand, the same way `.tab-panel`'s `gap: 12px` already is elsewhere) rather than left as now-stale numbers that happened to still produce *a* position, just not a correct one.

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop): `npm run build`/`tsc -b` clean; a text field's own floating strip (the "…" trigger alone) and, in Notes, the same strip with the User Action pair added (`extra`) both measured at the new 34px-square geometry with no visual regression — the strip still clears the field without overlapping it, the dropdown menu still opens directly under the trigger rather than overlapping it, and (for a `textarea`) the strip still sits clear of the scrollbar at the frame's own corner.

Rust-tested (`internal_clipboard.rs`): pushing adds to the top and peeking reads it without removing it (twice, to confirm); popping removes and returns the top entry, down to `None` once empty (the deadlock above, caught by this exact test); `set_all` replaces the whole stack in exactly the order given; too many entries or too much total text is refused with nothing already there disturbed; clearing empties it — 270 tests in all, passing together.

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP throughout): `npm run build`/`tsc -b` and `cargo build` both clean after the rename (every old `internal_clipboard_get`/`_set` caller across the app — `TextFieldMenu`, `DetailsModal`, `GoToPathModal`, both Settings pages, the sample app's own `_tab-lib.js`, `link_navigation.rs`'s own "copy the address" — updated to `push`/`peek`, not left as compatibility shims). The popup itself, opened from Settings over a real text field's own menu, both reached via the new 34px trigger (item 11 below): adding a blank entry landed it on top (confirmed against `internal_clipboard_list` directly, not just the DOM); editing it with real keystrokes (not a direct `.value` assignment — tried first, and silently did **not** reach the backend at all, since setting a controlled `<textarea>`'s `.value` property directly bypasses the tracked setter React's own `onChange` relies on, so nothing after "the DOM shows the right text" can be trusted without checking the backend too) persisted correctly on blur; removing one entry and clearing the rest both matched between the DOM and `internal_clipboard_list`. **Reordering needed a second real-input correction**: `ReorderList`'s own up/down arrows are wired to `onMouseDown` (to support holding one down to repeat), so a plain `element.click()` — which dispatches only a `click` event, never `mousedown`/`mouseup` — silently did nothing, read at first glance as "reordering doesn't work" when it was really a test artifact; real `Input.dispatchMouseEvent` press+release (this file's own `click.mjs`) moved the selected entry correctly, confirmed in both the DOM and the backend. **Opened from the "Go to a path…" box's own clipboard menu**, with real text selected in it: *Use this entry here* replaced the selection with the picked stack entry and closed the popup; *Replace this entry with the current selection*, re-opened after typing genuinely different text into the box and selecting it, correctly overwrote that one stack entry with the box's new text (confirmed via `internal_clipboard_list`, not assumed from a silent success).

**Pasting the OS clipboard's image as a file** (Notes' File Manager and Note Files Explorer, `NotesApp.tsx`'s `pasteClipboardImage`; `readOsClipboardImage` in `lib/clipboard.ts`): a toolbar button beside *Upload…* reads whatever image is on the system clipboard — a screenshot just taken, a picture copied from elsewhere — through `navigator.clipboard.read()`, offers a name to save it under (`clipboard-<timestamp>.<ext>`, the extension guessed from the image's MIME type: png/jpg/gif/webp/bmp/svg), and writes it the same way `uploadFiles` writes a picked file (`source.writeFromFile`, chunked, working the same for a local folder or a Filen account, in a branch too). No image found (empty clipboard, or text instead) says so rather than failing silently; reading it at all is subject to the same limits as the rest of `clipboard.ts` — thrown as a plain, readable error, not pretended away — and **Android's WebView can't read it here either**, same as the OS clipboard's text.
