**A fifth interval, the Note Archive Section, `109`→`101`** (asked for directly: "besides primary, secondary and ternary note sections, add an option for 'Note Archive Section' which will take values from 109-101"). Everything that works with a note's interval in this app — `NewNoteModal`'s radio list, `IndexesModal`'s per-interval select-group buttons and convert-to dropdown, `nextIndexIn`/`gapIndexesIn`/`normalize`/`convert`/`reorder`, the paste dialog's "keep its own interval"/"convert all to one" choices — is already written generically over `NOTE_INTERVALS` (`noteIndexes.ts`: `Object.entries(config.notes.numberings).map(...)`), so the whole feature is adding the fifth entry to the one config file both sides read (`config/folder-pairs-and-notes.json`'s `notes.numberings.archiveSection`, `{ "from": 109, "to": 101, "digits": 3, "indexing": "afterLargest" }` — the same `afterLargest` shape the three existing sections already use) and its matching field in `config.rs`'s `NoteNumberings` (`archive_section`) and `appConfig.ts`'s `AppConfig` interface (`archiveSection`) — not a new code path anywhere downstream. `config.rs`'s `validate` picks it up automatically into the five-way overlap check (`all[1..6]`, shifted from `all[1..5]` to make room) and the two-digit-internals check (now `all[6..]`), so a config mistake that made `101`–`109` collide with the primary sections' `111`–`199` (it doesn't — `101`–`109` sits entirely below `111`) would still be caught at the very first use, the same as any other overlap. **Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP, through the real UI, not by reading the generic code and assuming it generalizes)**: *New note…*'s radio list showed all five intervals including "Note Archive Section (109–101)"; creating a note in it landed its folder pair at `109` on disk (`afterLargest`'s own "the interval's start when none is used" rule, confirmed against a real `109`/`109-Archived Thing` pair beside the pre-existing `999`-numbered note); the indexes dialog's per-interval select buttons correctly showed "Note Archive Section (1)" once that note existed (and omitted every other empty interval, as `groups`' own filter already does); its convert-to dropdown listed "Note Archive Section (109→101)" alongside the other four; **converting the note from the Archive Section into Primary note sections moved it to `199` on disk** (the two-phase temporary-prefix rename, confirmed by the old `109` pair being gone and a new `199` pair present with the identical content), and **converting it back moved it to `109` again** — both directions of `convert` exercised against the new interval as source and target, not just one. Two new Rust assertions were added to the existing `config::tests` (not new test functions, since the existing ones already parse and validate the whole file): `the_shipped_file_is_valid_and_says_what_the_strategy_says` now also asserts `archive_section`'s `(from, to, digits)` and indexing match the spec, and `overlapping_numberings_and_bad_values_are_refused` now also confirms a deliberately-overlapping Archive Section (made to collide with the primary sections) is refused the same way the existing primary/note-items overlap case already is. `cargo test --lib` (276 tests, unchanged count — only existing test bodies grew) and `npx tsc --noEmit` both clean.

**The interval radio list — in `NewNoteModal.tsx` and `PasteNotesModal.tsx`'s own "Convert all to one interval" — read as a row, not a list; asked for directly: "align them vertically instead of horizontally."** Both wrap their `<label>`/radio options in a plain `<fieldset className="notes-field">`, reusing `.notes-field`'s own look (a bordered legend/field pair) — but `.notes-field` itself is `display: inline-flex` with the default `row` direction, meant for a single label-beside-its-input (`<span>Title</span><input/>`); on a `<fieldset>` wrapping *several* option rows instead, every row became a flex item of that same row and sat side by side. **Fixed** by scoping an override to the tag — `fieldset.notes-field { display: flex; flex-direction: column; align-items: flex-start; gap: 4px; }` (`notes.css`, right beside `.notes-field`'s own rule) — more specific than the class-only selector, so the plain `<label className="notes-field">` used everywhere else for an ordinary single field is completely untouched; only the two fieldsets (both lists of options, nothing else in the app giving `.notes-field` to a `<fieldset>`) pick it up. Verified live (isolated **debug** data folder, desktop, real CDP, through the real UI): `NewNoteModal`'s five intervals measured at the identical `left` with strictly increasing `top` (367→388→409→430→451, 21px apart) — confirmed both by direct `getBoundingClientRect()` reads and a screenshot showing a clean vertical list; `PasteNotesModal`'s own identical fieldset (reached by copying a real test note and opening "Copy … here…" with "Convert all to one interval" checked) measured the same way, five options stacked one per line. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npm run build` both clean; no Rust change, so `cargo test --lib` doesn't apply.

**Add Folders Pair** (the File Manager and the Note Files Explorer, `FolderPairModal.tsx`, `lib/folderPairs.ts`): a dialog with the **title**, the **short folder's name** (the first free index by default, or any text a folder can be called — what a folder can't have is dropped) and a checkbox to make **a markdown file inside the short folder** (named like a note's, starting with the title as a heading). The title becomes the readable half of the full folder's name **the way a note's title does** (`namePartFromTitle`): the characters a file name can't have are **stripped** (an info icon lists them: `< > : " \ | ? *` and control characters), `/` becomes `%`, `%` becomes `%%`, cut to the config's longest part; the full folder holds the `.keep` (with the config's content). The dialog says what it will make, and refuses a name that is taken.

**Renaming a file or a folder as in Total Commander** (the File Manager and the Note Files Explorer, list and thumbnails): **F2** on the focused item (or the *Rename* button — the mouse / touch way) turns its name into a box with the name *without its extension* selected; **Up and Down submit the new name and go on to rename the item before / after**; Enter or a press elsewhere submits, Esc cancels.
- **Notes settings** (`NotesSettingsPage.tsx`, `settings.ts`, key `notes.settings`; a place `v=settings`, reached from the home page): *show the notes' indexes* (on by default) and the app's own clipboard — how many characters it holds, and **Clear** (`internal_clipboard_clear`, which every window may call).
- **Go to a path** (`GoToPathModal`) keeps a query (`parsePathInput` → `{ segments, query }`): a file's address shows the view of its kind — the editor for a text file — and a note's address opens the note.
- Tested live on desktop (isolated data folder): a notebook made by hand, creating a note (its pair of folders and files on disk), editing and saving with the heading changed (marker folder, markdown file and both JSON files renamed), the note as a web app in a window, saving from the editor reloading that window, then (tab-hosted, isolated data folder, desktop and the Android emulator) a click on a note showing it in a second tab of the same window, a second click bringing that tab back (still two tabs, no window added), the page refused `open_file_as_web_app` and `save_to_device` yet allowed the clipboard and an app state of its own, the three-dots menu (index changed on disk with its marker, select by menu and by right click / long press, a separate tab, show/suspend/close of the syncing tab), the settings page (indexes hidden, clipboard cleared), and — desktop — a save in an editor in another window reloading the syncing tab and not the separate one; a child note, the note's files explorer (`01` made, a file made inside it, *Open in File Manager*), a note's address in *Go to a path*.

In a **Filen** source Notes also does the following (all through the cache commands below): **saving asks Filen first** (`filen_cache_check_version`) — if the file is no longer the version that was opened, nothing is written and a dialog offers *Overwrite*, *Discard changes* (the edits are lost and the file is read again) or *Cancel* (back to the editor, as if Save had not been pressed); the editor has a **New branch…** button (makes a branch and carries on in it: an unchanged file is checked out there, an edited one is saved into it with the next Save); each Filen file has a **lock** button (locked against caching) and, in a branch, a **check out** button (and *let go* for a file that was checked out and not changed) — see "Versions, locks and checkouts" below. The admin-app's own Filen tab has no editor; when an upload would replace a file of the same name it says so first.

**Editing a text file is part of the tab's place**: a tab whose editor is open has `&e=<file>` in its resource id (the file, relative to its source's root), its resource type is `editing` (its own icon), and its second row reads "Editing /file" plus, in italics, "unsaved changes" — so the window manager shows what each Notes tab is doing. Activating such a tab (or reloading the window) opens the editor on that file again, and switching to a tab that isn't editing closes it. **The unsaved text is kept as a draft** (`notes.drafts` in the app state: tab guid → source, branch, path, text, version), written a moment after the last key and when the tab is left, restored with the editor and dropped when the file is saved, its changes are discarded or the person closes the editor; the version check before saving still applies to a restored draft. A tab that names no place of its own starts from the last place but never in the editor.

## The Filen cache (`files/`)

`files_cache.rs` (the `Cache`, in `HostState`-style managed state), with `filen_cache.rs` as its commands (`filen_cache_account/_set_interval/_clear/_list/_read/_write/_mkdir/_rm/_rename/_branches/_create_branch/_branch_changes/_commit_branch/_discard_branch`, same shapes as the `filen_*` ones, plus an optional `branch`; no credential ever reaches a window). Layout inside `files/` (built with folder pairs — see below):

```
files/data.db                     listings, metadata, per-account settings, branch changes
files/a/NNN-filen@@<email>@@<id>/ only a .keep: says whose NNN is
files/a/NNN/c/…                   cached CONTENT of the account, at its Filen paths
files/a/NNN/b/MMM/…               what branch MMM of the account has changed (MMM-<branch name> beside it)
files/a/NNN/t/…                   thumbnails of the account's files (see "Media")
files/a/NNN/tb/MMM/…              thumbnails of the files branch MMM changed (goes with the branch)
```

**Everything of one account lives inside its own `NNN` folder in `a`** — its content, its branches, its thumbnails and its branches'
thumbnails — not spread across top-level folders of their own; a folder of this device's branches and thumbnails live inside its `NNN`
in `a` the same way (see "Branches of a folder of this device"). **An older layout kept `b`, `t` and `tb` as top-level folders beside
`a`** (each with its own `NNN-<owner>` pairs); `Cache::open` **migrates** whatever it finds there into the owner's folder in `a` the first
time it runs (`migrate_old_layout`: the folder is renamed in place, never copied, so a big branch isn't duplicated; a target that already
exists is left alone; the old top-level folders are removed once empty) — safe to run again, and it works for a folder of this device's old
pairs (`local-fs@@…`) the same way it does for a Filen account's. Rust-tested (a hand-built old layout, migrated, checked on disk, and
migrated again to confirm nothing more happens) and verified live on desktop: an old-layout `files/` folder made by hand had its branch,
its thumbnails and its branch's thumbnails all found inside the root's own folder in `a` after the next start, with the old `b`/`t`/`tb`
gone.

- **A cache, not a sync.** Nothing is fetched until asked: listing a folder fetches and remembers that listing; opening or exporting a file fetches and remembers its content. Everything remembered — listings, metadata and contents — expires after the account's **cache interval** (default 1 hour, chosen per account in Notes) and is then fetched again; with the interval turned off (`ttlSecs` = `null`) nothing ever expires, which is how a person makes things available offline. If Filen can't be reached, an expired listing/file is served marked *stale* rather than failing. Writes to the account itself go straight to Filen and update the cache.
- **Branches** are the git-like alternative: a set of local changes (files written, folders made, things deleted) kept under `b/` and in `data.db` until *committed* (applied to Filen, then the branch is deleted) or *discarded*. Reading through a branch shows Filen's cached state with the branch's changes on top. A commit first checks that Filen still looks as it did when each change was made; if not, it applies nothing and returns the `conflicts` unless `force`. A branch's name is any valid file name of ≤ 100 characters and is its folder's readable half.
- **Versions, locks and checkouts** (`files_cache.rs`, commands `filen_cache_version/_check_version/_rebase/_set_locked/_checkout/_release`):
  - A file's **version** is what Filen's listing says: whether it exists, its size and modification time (`FileVersion`). `filen_cache_version` gives the one the cache knows — what a window that has just opened the file is working on (in a branch that changed or checked out the file: the version *that* was based on). **`filen_cache_check_version` asks Filen itself, not the cache** (a locked file would keep the cache frozen), and compares it with the version the caller remembers: `{ upToDate, problem, current }` (*newer*, *different*, *deleted after you started*, *created after you started*); it fails when Filen can't be reached — the account can't be written to then anyway, and Notes lets a save into a *branch* go ahead (it stays local, and the commit checks again). In a branch, a person who chose to overwrite calls `filen_cache_rebase`, which bases the change on what Filen has now so the commit doesn't warn about the same thing again.
  - **Commit asks Filen directly too** (a fresh listing of each folder involved, no longer through the cache), and it now verifies **every** file in the branch, checkouts included: a file that changed or was deleted in Filen after it was changed *or checked out* is a conflict, reported unless `force`.
  - A **lock** (`file_locks` table, kept apart from `entries` so it survives its row being dropped and made again) freezes a file of the account: its cached copy is served as it is — no check that it is current, so it opens offline too — and neither the cache interval, a newer listing, a deletion in Filen, nor "Clear the cache" touches it (the folders above it stay too, their rows name it). Locking fetches the file first if it isn't cached (there has to be a copy to keep). It belongs to the **account's** file, so a branch that reads it through sees the same frozen copy, and the lock is available in the account and in every branch; writing to a locked file (or uploading over it) keeps it locked, on the new content, and deleting or moving it, or disconnecting the account, drops the lock. `Entry.locked` says so in listings.
  - A **checkout** (`filen_cache_checkout`, in a branch) takes a file into the branch *without changing it*: its content is copied into the branch and recorded as a change of kind `checkout`, based on the version that was copied, so it appears among the branch's pending changes. Editing it turns it into an ordinary `put` that keeps that base; `filen_cache_release` lets go of one that was never changed. Committing applies nothing for a checkout — it only had its version verified.
- **Lifecycle.** Connecting an account creates its two folders (`ensure_account`); disconnecting it (`filen_logout`) deletes its folders in `a`, `b`, `t` and `tb` — its cached files, every branch and their thumbnails — and its rows in `files/data.db` (`forget_account`); "Delete app data" and "clear the custom data folder" remove `files/` with the rest (after closing the cache's database — `Cache::close` — since Windows can't delete an open file), and relocating the data folder switches to the `files/` inside the new one, exactly as it does for `admin/`.
- The whole thing is tested without a network (`Remote` trait, in-memory remote): TTL expiry, offline stale serving, write-through, branch overlay/commit/conflict/discard, lifecycle. Live-verified on desktop **against a real Filen account** (the cache commands and the Notes UI, inside a throwaway folder in the account): folder/file write-through checked against Filen directly, a cache hit versus a forced refresh, the interval expiring and "never", rename, a branch that is committed, a real conflict (refused without `force`, the branch wins with it), a discarded branch, gap-filled branch indexes and the resulting `files/a` / `files/b` layout on disk. Also driven through the Notes UI with the `filen_*` IPC faked (the stale/offline indicator, which needs Filen to be unreachable). **Not** run live: disconnecting an account (it would log the person out; covered by unit tests), offline serving, and the Android build against a real account.

**Big files never sit in memory** (one chunk at a time is the most anything holds), on the way up, down and out:
- **Down.** A file is fetched from Filen chunk by chunk (`filen::ops::read_file_chunks`, 1 MiB at a time) into a `.part` file beside its cache location and **renamed into place only when complete** (`Cache::cached_file`); an interrupted download leaves no partial file and nothing marked as cached. `Cache::read` (the bytes API, for the editor's ≤ 2 MB) is built on it; Notes doesn't even fetch a file above 2 MB to edit it.
- **Up.** `begin_upload` / `upload_bytes` / `finish_upload` (`filen::ops::UploadSession`) cut whatever is pushed into exact 1 MiB Filen chunks, encrypt and send each as it completes, and only `finish_upload` makes the file exist (replacing an existing file by trashing the old one once the new one is readable). The cache wraps it in an `UploadJob` that also writes every piece to a temporary file in the account's `tmp/` folder, which *becomes the cached copy* when the upload finishes — Filen and the cache get each piece at the same moment. Dropping an unfinished job deletes its temporary file, and anything in flight when the app stopped is cleared at the next start (`remove_leftover_uploads`).
- **Two ways to feed an upload.** (1) *From the page*, for a `File` from the device's file chooser (which has no path): `filen_cache_upload_begin` → `filen_cache_upload_chunk` (the piece is the request body, any size) → `filen_cache_upload_finish` / `_abort`; a session belongs to the window that began it (another window's push, finish or abort is refused/ignored) and lapses after an hour. (2) *From a file the app may already read*: `filen_cache_upload_from_path(…, root, source)` — Rust reads it piece by piece; the file never goes through the window. Into a branch, either way, the file stays local until the branch is committed, and `commit_branch` streams each changed file from disk too.
- **Out.** `filen_cache_export` (to the device) and `filen_cache_download_to(…, root, dest)` (into a folder the app may use) fetch into the cache as above, then `fs::copy` on this side; `export_local_file` does the same for a local file. On Android the export streams into Downloads through MediaStore (`DeviceFiles.saveFileToDownloads`). Notes' copy/paste between sources uses these when one side is local and the other Filen, and `fs_copy` (a plain `std::fs::copy` between two places the scope allows, refusing to copy a file onto itself) when both are local — the Files tab's copy too; only the admin-app's own Filen tab (which doesn't use the cache) still moves a whole file through the page.
- **Into a local folder** the same idea, for a `File` from the device's file chooser (`fs_upload.rs`, the local counterpart of the Filen sessions): `fs_upload_begin(root, path)` → `fs_upload_chunk` (4 MiB pieces, request body + `id`) → `fs_upload_finish` / `_abort`, in `src/lib/fs.ts` `uploadFile` (used by Notes' local sources and the Files tab). Pieces are appended to a temporary file in `files/local-uploads/` — the app's own folder, never the person's — and renamed (or, across drives, copied) into place at the end, so the file appears only when complete; the destination is judged by the scope at the start *and* at the end (a folder forgotten meanwhile is refused), the upload belongs to its window, and the folder is emptied at every start. Verified live with a 96 MiB file into the user folder and a picked folder: byte-identical, 1.4 s each, and the backend's memory rose by a fixed ~25 MB (the pieces in flight), not with the size.
- Verified live against a real account with a 48 MiB file: byte-identical round trips through both upload routes and the streamed download, and the backend process's memory stayed within a few MB of idle (working set 41 → 47 MB during the upload, 45 MB during the download) instead of growing with the file.

**Folder pairs** (`folder_pairs.rs`; the strategy is in `docs/strategies/folder-pairs-strategy.md`): to make a folder that a program can address by a short name and a person can identify by a long one, create two siblings — `NNN` (the data folder; three digits, zero-padded) and `NNN-<full name part>` (a marker whose name says what `NNN` is, holding nothing but a `.keep` file with one dash in it, so it is never an *empty* folder — copying, mirroring and archiving tend to lose those; `create` and `ensure` write it, and the cache's `open` gives it to every pair at each start, which is how pairs made before the rule got theirs). `NNN` is worked out from a listing of the parent (never remembered), by the **`Numbering`** the caller uses (`Numbering::DEFAULT`: no prefix, upward from `001` with three digits, first free — in general a constant **prefix**, an **interval** of indexes going up or down, or a single constant index, and an **`Indexing`** option; `rename`/`reassign` renumber many pairs in two phases through the temporary prefix `t_`; see the strategy document, "Generalising the way the index is computed") — its **`Indexing`** option: `AfterLargest` (one more than the highest `NNN-…`; gaps are left, only an index freed by deleting the newest pair returns) or **`FillGaps`** (the lowest index, from 1, that isn't in use — `001` and `003` are followed by `002`; a bare short folder `NNN` with no full folder beside it counts as in use). **Everything we create now fills gaps** (`files_cache::NUMBERING`): accounts in `files/a`, account folders in `files/b` and branches — so deleting a pair leaves no permanent hole. One consequence to remember: a branch's index is what a Notes tab's resource id names (`b=<index>`), and a discarded branch's index can be handed to a *new* branch, so a stale tab that still names it would open the new one (a tab whose branch vanished, and whose index wasn't reused, falls back to the account). For accounts the part is `filen@@<email>@@<user id>`; for branches it is the branch's name. Use it for anything new that follows the same need.

## Media: the viewer, thumbnails, and serving files in pieces

**Serving a file in pieces** (`file_serving.rs`; used by the `csuser` protocol and, on Android, by `nativeServe`). A `<video>`/`<audio>` asks for its file with a `Range` header and seeks by asking for other ranges, so a request with a range is answered `206 Partial Content` with **at most 4 MiB** read from the file at that place (`Content-Range`, `Accept-Ranges: bytes`; a range past the end is `416`); the player asks again as it goes, so nothing bigger than a piece is ever held — in Rust, in the webview or (Android) in the JVM. A request with no range gets the whole file, as before (a picture, a script, a page). Every source is a file on disk by then: the user folder, a picked folder, or — for Filen — the cache's copy (`Cache::cached_file` fetches it there piece by piece first, so playing a Filen video starts once it is downloaded, and never holds it in memory). **Across origins**: Notes (`tauri.localhost`) loads files from the web apps' origin (`csuser`), so the CSP's `img-src` and `media-src` — and only those — name `csuser:` and `http://csuser.localhost` (the CSP test allows them for those two directives only), and an answer carries `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` **only for this app's own frontend origins** (`allowed_origin`; on Android, where the WebView doesn't always say where a request comes from, a request with no `Origin` is taken as the app's own) — so a thumbnail can be read back from a canvas, while a web page of the web, in an external site's window, can read nothing. `media_url(file)` gives a page the address for a `FileRef` — segment by segment, so a `#` or `?` in a name is part of the name, and never a real path. Tested with a 600 MB file: opening it moves the process's memory by nothing.

**Playing a Filen `.mkv` once broke the whole account's file manager, and playing a local one froze the whole window — reported live, together**: "I tried playing an .mkv file from the Notes File Manager connected to my filen account: … then there was only a blank screen … after this issue, the file manager no longer worked because the listings would take forever" — and, separately, a local `.mkv` "did now play it, [but] the app's window froze (the action buttons and controls no longer appeared when I clicked)" until it was force-closed from the OS's own window controls. Two independent, real concurrency bugs, not one:

- **A per-account lock held across an unbounded network download.** `Cache`'s own `locks` (one `tokio::sync::Mutex` per account, `files_cache.rs`) used to be taken by `list`/`read`/`cached_file` and held for the *whole* call — metadata **and** the file's actual download — and there is no HTTP timeout anywhere in the Filen client, so a stuck or merely slow fetch held the lock indefinitely. Every *other* operation on that same account (an unrelated folder's listing, a different file's read) serializes on that one lock, so one stuck video download queued the entire file manager behind it — exactly "the listings would take forever." Worse, `cached_file_account`'s download always `std::fs::File::create`s a fresh `.part` file (truncating any earlier attempt) with no check for "already in progress" — so a `<video>` element's own normal behavior of issuing several overlapping Range probes while it buffers made each probe independently restart the whole download from scratch, which could starve out the download forever if probes kept arriving faster than one full download could finish — matching "blank screen, progress wasn't moving."

  **Fixed by splitting the account-wide lock's scope.** A second, narrower lock, `download_locks` — keyed by `(user_id, path)`, not just `user_id` — now guards *only* the download itself; the account lock is held just for the metadata steps (the locked-and-cached shortcut, the listing refresh, the already-cached check — factored into `cached_file_account_metadata`, shared by both paths) and released before the network call. A plain file read (`read`/`cached_file`'s `None` branch — what media serving and the editor go through) now calls a new `cached_file_account_unlocked`, which takes the account lock only for that metadata step, then the narrower download lock for the fetch; two concurrent callers for the *same* file now coalesce there (the second waits for the first, then finds the file already on disk, rather than restarting it) instead of each truncating the other's `.part` file. **`hard_refresh`, `set_locked`, `checkout` and `cached_file_branch` are deliberately left alone** — they already hold the account lock for their own larger sequence of steps and call the original, always-locked `cached_file_account` (unchanged), since none of them was implicated in this report and their own atomicity still needs it.

  **A real deadlock was found by the test suite, not by inspection, building this**: a first version had the *new* `cached_file_account` take its own account lock internally (reasoning it would only ever be called unlocked) — but `hard_refresh`/`set_locked`/`checkout` all call it while *already* holding that same lock (`tokio::sync::Mutex` isn't reentrant), so every "locked file" test (`a_locked_file_is_frozen_until_it_is_unlocked` and seven siblings) hung forever the moment `cargo test --lib` reached them — caught live as `cargo test`'s own "has been running for over 60 seconds" warnings, not a clean failure. Fixed by keeping the originally-locked `cached_file_account` genuinely unchanged (no internal locking at all) and adding the new, separately-named `cached_file_account_unlocked` purely as an alternative entry point for the two callers that don't already hold the lock.

  Rust-tested (`files_cache::tests`, with a `MemoryRemote` test hook — `gate_next_download` — that holds the *next* `download()` call open until told to proceed, standing in for a stuck network request): `a_slow_download_does_not_block_an_unrelated_listing_or_read` — a gated download of one file running concurrently (`tokio::join!`) with a listing and a different file's own read, both of which finish well before the gate is ever released; `concurrent_downloads_of_the_same_file_coalesce_instead_of_restarting` — two concurrent reads of the *same* gated file, the second queued on the download lock rather than racing an independent download of its own, and only one real `download()` call (`remote.reads == 1`) for both. All 255 tests (every pre-existing one, locked-file tests included) pass together.

- **Plain synchronous file I/O run directly on the async runtime's own shared worker threads.** `file_serving::respond_file` — `std::fs::File::open`, `.read_to_end`, `.seek`/`.read_exact` — is a plain, deliberately synchronous function (so its own unit tests below need no async runtime at all), but its three real callers (`lib.rs`'s `serve_file`, for the `csuser://` protocol's ordinary files, and `notes_pages.rs`'s `serve`, for a picked folder's or Filen's own file) used to call it directly inside an `async fn`, on whichever Tokio worker thread was running that task. A `<video>` element's ordinary behavior — several concurrent, overlapping Range requests while it buffers — can occupy every worker thread at once with blocking disk reads, starving every *other* task on the runtime, IPC command dispatch included: the mechanism behind "the app's window froze … buttons … no longer appeared" for a *local* file, where there's no network and so no account lock to blame — the freeze was the runtime itself, not the Filen cache.

  **Fixed with `tauri::async_runtime::spawn_blocking`.** `file_serving::respond_file_blocking(path, meta, csp)` (new) runs the unchanged, still-synchronous `respond_file` on a blocking-task thread instead of a shared worker thread, and is what the three real call sites now go through (`serve_file` and `notes_pages::serve`'s two branches became `async` only for this reason); `respond_file`'s own tests still call it directly, synchronously, unaffected.

  Rust-tested: the existing `file_serving::tests` (`a_range_is_read_and_cut_to_a_piece` and siblings) all still pass unchanged, confirming `respond_file`'s own logic wasn't touched. **Verified live** (isolated data folder, desktop, over the real app's own WebView2 CDP port): a genuine 120 MiB local file in the user folder, opened by a second window's own page (`csuser` origin, same-origin `fetch()` with `Range` headers — the same mechanism a real `<video>` element uses), with **60 concurrent 4 MiB Range requests fired at once** (240 MiB in flight) while the *admin window*'s own IPC (`get_app_state`, timed round-trip) was polled every 150 ms from a separate window. Before this fix existed in the build under test, this is exactly the shape of load a real `<video>`'s buffering produces at smaller scale; with the fix, IPC round trips measured **3–15 ms except for one call that landed inside the heaviest moment of contention (901–957 ms, reproduced twice)**, recovering to single digits immediately after — nowhere near the multi-second total freeze the report described, and the 60 reads themselves all completed correctly (`206 Partial Content`, exactly 4 MiB each) in ~1.2–1.3 s. A direct, controlled before/after comparison (temporarily bypassing `spawn_blocking` to show the same burst *without* the fix) was attempted but abandoned partway through: relaunching the isolated app to pick up the bypassed build landed on a *shared* WebView2 browser process left over from the previous launch (a known WebView2 behavior — `WEBVIEW2_ADDITIONAL_BROWSER_ARGUMENTS`, including the CDP debugging port, only takes effect on the browser process's first creation for a given profile), and the only way found to force a fresh one would have meant killing `msedgewebview2.exe` processes system-wide — including ones that could belong to other, unrelated running apps — which wasn't a safe thing to do unprompted. The "after" measurement above stands on its own (a real, reproduced, sub-second worst case against a deliberately heavy synthetic load), and the mechanism itself — blocking I/O starving an async runtime's shared worker pool — is well-established Tokio behavior, not something in doubt. **Not verified live**: the Filen-side symptom (the account-lock fix above) against a *real* Filen account's slow/stuck download — no live Filen account was available in this isolated instance; that half rests on the dedicated Rust tests, which model the exact "stuck download, concurrent listing" shape directly.

**The window-freeze fix above wasn't actually the whole story — reported live again, against the real file**: the person's own real 2.7 GB local `.mkv` still froze its viewer window black and unresponsive from just *opening* it (no play press) on both desktop and Android, after the `spawn_blocking` fix had already shipped. The remaining cause: `content_type_for` (`lib.rs`) maps every video extension the frontend recognizes as playable (`lib/media.ts`'s `mediaKindOf`, `video: ['mp4', 'm4v', 'webm', 'ogv', 'mov', 'mkv']`) to a real `video/*` MIME type — except `.mkv`, which fell through to the generic default, `application/octet-stream`. Chromium's `<video>` element decides whether to use its efficient, range-based progressive-loading pipeline based on the response's declared content type; an unrecognized/generic one is plausibly treated as an opaque, non-streamable blob, falling back to a strategy that needs the *whole* resource before anything can show — catastrophic for a multi-gigabyte file. **Fixed** by adding `"mkv" => "video/x-matroska"` to `content_type_for` — one line, the same table every one of `respond_file`'s three real call sites already shares.

**Along the way the person asked a sharper, more important question directly: does any of this ever load a whole video into RAM?** Looking again at `respond_file` with that question in mind: a *ranged* request (what a `<video>` element sends once it knows the server supports ranges) is correctly capped at `MAX_PIECE` (4 MiB) regardless of file size — but a request with **no** `Range` header reads the *entire* file into one `Vec<u8>` (`Vec::with_capacity(len as usize)` + `read_to_end`) before responding, which for a multi-gigabyte file would be a multi-gigabyte allocation and a long synchronous read. This is real, latent, and not yet fixed as its own item — but it is **not what was causing this particular bug**: verified live and conclusively (below), real `<video>` playback never actually hits that branch, because Chromium sends `Range: bytes=0-...` from its very first request once the content type reads as real video, so the ranged path (already bounded) is the one actually exercised. Whether to still harden the no-range path as a general safety margin (it's reachable by *some* client, even if not a real `<video>` element in current practice) is an open, separate question — not required by this bug, but a reasonable thing to tighten given how directly asked.

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real WebView2 CDP, **the person's own real file** — hard-linked, not copied, from its real location into the isolated instance's user folder, same volume, so the test never touched or duplicated the original; confirmed untouched afterward by size): clicking the real 2.7 GB (2,925,274,205-byte) `.mkv` in Notes' File Manager, through the actual UI (a real DOM `.click()` on the row, not a direct backend call) — the viewer opened with full working chrome (title, prev/next, fullscreen, close) and a correctly populated **45:18 duration**, `video.readyState` reaching `4` (`HAVE_ENOUGH_DATA`) within about a second, `networkState: 1` (idle, not stuck fetching) — the black frame at that point was simply "paused at 0:00," not a freeze. Seeking to 2:08 and calling `.play()` produced real decoded 1080p frames (`videoWidth: 1920, videoHeight: 1080`), confirmed visually via a real screenshot showing actual picture content, not a blank/black viewer. **Directly answering the RAM question**: the backend process's working set measured **43 MB** both before opening the file and during active playback — not the 2.7 GB the no-range path could have allocated — and the admin window's own IPC (`get_app_state`) stayed instantly responsive throughout. The synthetic test from the entry above (raw `fetch()` calls with explicit `Range` headers) had validated the backend's concurrency fix correctly but never exercised a real `<video>` element's own content-type-dependent fetch strategy — which is exactly where this second bug was hiding, and why a second, real-file, real-UI verification pass mattered here rather than trusting the first one's green result to cover this too.

Rust-tested (`lib.rs`): `every_recognized_video_extension_gets_a_real_video_content_type` asserts all six of `mediaKindOf`'s video extensions (not just `.mkv`) resolve to a `video/*` content type, so this exact gap can't silently reopen for a different extension later.

**The media viewer** (`components/MediaViewer.tsx`, `lib/media.ts`: `mediaKindOf` by extension — pictures png/jpg/gif/webp/bmp/svg/avif/ico, video mp4/m4v/webm/ogv/mov/mkv, sound mp3/m4a/aac/wav/ogg/opus/flac, as far as the webview plays them). In Notes' **File Manager and Note Files Explorer** (both are `NotesApp`) a click on such a file — or its *View* button (every media file also has *Edit as text*, see "The file editor" above) — opens it over the whole window, with the folder's other media a step away (arrows in the bar, PageUp/PageDown, ← → for pictures). The file is never read into the page: it is an address the webview loads itself.
- **Picture**: opens showing the whole of it. Zoom by the wheel (about the pointer), a two-finger pinch, the buttons, +/−/0 or a double click; drag it when it is bigger than the window — it is kept from being dragged off (`clamp`: centred while smaller than the window on an axis, no gap at either edge once larger), so zoomed in enough it fills the window. **One press on it hides the bars, the next brings them back.**
- **Video / sound**: a bar with play/pause, **back and forward 10 seconds — acting on `pointerdown`**, the time, a draggable progress, sound on/off. Playing a video **hides the bars**; a press on the video brings them back and, if it keeps playing, they go again after 3 s (the timer restarts with any touch of the bar); pausing keeps them. A sound keeps its bar.
- **Full screen** for any of them (F, or the button; Esc leaves it): `requestFullscreen()` on the viewer — on Android through `WindowActivity`'s `onShowCustomView` (see "Android").
- Tested live on desktop and the Android emulator (isolated data): the whole picture at first, the wheel/buttons zooming to 7×+, a drag (synthetic pointer events on desktop, a real swipe on Android) that stops at the edges, the bars toggling by presses, the next media, full screen (and Back leaving it first on Android), a sound playing and seeking through ranges, ±10 s, a recorded video playing with its bars hiding/returning/staying when paused.

**Remembered playback position** (asked for directly: "for video and audio players let's remember the user's progress per each file"). `lib/mediaProgress.ts` keeps one `{ time, at }` entry per file, keyed by a stable identity built from its `FileRef` (never a real path — `user:<path>`, `device:<rootId>:<path>` or `filen:<userId>:<branch>:<path>`), under Notes' own app state (`notes.mediaProgress` in `data.db`, the same storage every other remembered place already uses — see "App state storage" — never browser storage). Bounded at 300 entries (the oldest `at` dropped first), so watching a lot of different files over the app's whole lifetime doesn't grow it forever. `MediaViewer.tsx` stays host-agnostic about *how* this is stored, the same way it already is about `downloadProgress`/`downloadError`: three new, all-optional props — `initialTime(item, duration)` (asked for once `duration` is known, so a saved position can be judged against it), `onProgress(item, time)` (fired now and then while playing — throttled to once every 5 s, `PROGRESS_REPORT_MS` — and once more, bypassing the throttle, on pause, on letting go of a drag on the progress bar, and when the component unmounts: closing the viewer, switching to another item, or the item finishing) and `onFinished(item)` (the item played to its own natural end) — with `NotesApp.tsx` the only caller that wires them to the three real functions (`mediaProgress`/`saveMediaProgress`/`clearMediaProgress`). **A saved position within 5 seconds of the file's own duration is treated as already finished** (`NEAR_END_SECONDS`) and not offered as a place to resume — otherwise a video watched to the end would "resume" one awkward seek-back away from where it already finished. Pictures are untouched; this only ever applies to `PlayerStage` (video/audio).

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP) against the person's own real 2.7 GB `.mkv` (the same file "Media: the viewer…" above already uses for its concurrency/content-type fixes — hard-linked, not copied, into the isolated instance, confirmed untouched afterward): opened fresh, `currentTime` started at `0` (nothing saved yet); seeked to 500 s, played briefly and paused — `get_app_state('notes.mediaProgress')` read back `{"user:Umbre.S01E01.mkv":{"time":500.31,...}}` immediately, confirming the pause-triggered flush bypasses the 5 s throttle; closed and reopened the same file — `currentTime` came back at exactly `500.310275`, the saved value, confirming the resume path; seeked to 2 seconds before the end, played briefly, paused, closed and reopened — this time `currentTime` was `0`, confirming the near-the-end position was correctly recognized as "finished" and not offered; let the video play for real, uninterrupted, all the way to its own genuine `ended` event (seeked to 0.3 s before the end first, so this didn't mean actually waiting out the full 45 minutes) — `get_app_state` read back `{}` immediately after, confirming `onFinished` cleared the entry; and, separately, seeked to 100 s and left it **playing without ever pausing** — six seconds later, with `currentTime` live at ~111.8 s, the saved value had already advanced to ~110.1 s on its own, confirming the periodic (non-pause-triggered) throttled report works independently of the flush-on-stop paths. **Not separately exercised live**: a real audio file (none was at hand in the isolated instance) and the Android build — both go through the identical `PlayerStage` code path already proven above, with nothing kind-specific or platform-specific in the new logic itself.

**A video could open to a completely black, un-paintable window — reported live again, after the `.mkv` concurrency and content-type fixes above, on the same real file**: the person's own description was decisive — "the controls are just invisible but clickable and hoverable" — which rules out a hang or a crash outright (the DOM, React, and every click handler were all working correctly) and points instead at a pure *rendering* bug: something painted on top of the real content without actually being there, or the real content simply never got presented to the screen. This is **`MicrosoftEdge/WebView2Feedback#5574`**, a known WebView2/DirectComposition bug: when a `<video>` element's own painted box matches its host window's client area to within 2px, together with bottom-anchored absolutely-positioned siblings, the OS compositor can stop presenting *any* fresh pixels for the whole window — not just the video — while the WebView2 surface underneath keeps rendering correctly the entire time (confirmed by the bug report's own finding that `Page.captureScreenshot` over CDP never reproduces it, since that capture path reads the surface directly and bypasses the real OS-compositor presentation step the bug actually lives in). `.media-viewer` (`position: fixed; inset: 0`) and `.media-stage` (`position: absolute; inset: 0`) both deliberately fill the entire window — exactly the trigger shape — with `.media-bar-bottom` sitting right at the bottom edge.

**This is also why three separate live reproductions in this same investigation — the plain user folder, the real picked-folder path matching the person's exact setup, checked both immediately and after several seconds, zero JS exceptions or console errors every time — all looked completely fine**: CDP screenshots are structurally blind to this exact bug, so a clean CDP-driven pass is not evidence against it, only evidence that nothing *else* (no crash, no stuck fetch, no exception) was also going wrong. Getting this diagnosis right needed the person's own eyes on the real screen, not another round of remote-debugging automation.

**Fixed** with the documented, minimal mitigation: `.media-video` is inset by `calc(100% - 4px)` (2px on every side, centred by `.media-stage`'s existing flexbox centering — no other change needed) rather than a flush `100% × 100%`, which is enough to break the exact-match trigger (the same report found 1px insufficient, 2px reliable). The alternative, heavier fixes the same report lists — `--disable-gpu-compositing`/`--disable-direct-composition` as WebView2 browser arguments — were not used: they move the *whole app's* compositing off the GPU path, a much bigger tradeoff than a 2px CSS inset nobody can see. `.media-image` (pictures) and `.media-sound` (audio, no decoded video surface to overlay) are not affected by this specific bug and were left unchanged.

**Not verified live by this session's own tooling, and structurally can't be**: the fix addresses exactly the presentation layer `Page.captureScreenshot` cannot see, so no CDP-based check — however many times repeated — can confirm or refute it. This one needs the person's own on-screen confirmation after deploying.

**Thumbnails** (*View thumbnails* in the File Manager's toolbar, `ThumbnailGrid.tsx`; the choice is `viewThumbnails` in Notes' settings). A grid of cards — the small image of a picture or of a video's first moments, an icon for everything else; a press opens (folder, viewer, editor), the card's three dots / right click / long press offer details, export, copy, cut, rename, delete (`menuFor`). A card makes its thumbnail when it comes into view (`thumbnails.ts`: the webview decodes the file — a picture, or a video seeked a moment in — and draws it to a 256 px JPEG; three at a time; a file that can't be decoded, or a Filen file over 32 MiB that isn't cached yet, keeps its icon).
- **On this device** thumbnails live for the session only (blob addresses, keyed by path, date and size); the files are right there.
- **In a Filen account** they are kept on disk with the cache, inside the account's own folder in `a`, beside its `c` and `b`: `files/a/NNN/t/…` (**t**humbnails of the account's files) and `files/a/NNN/tb/MMM/…` (of the files **b**ranch `MMM` changed), folder pairs like the branches' (`layout::FILES_THUMBNAILS_FOLDER`, `FILES_BRANCH_THUMBNAILS_FOLDER`; `Cache::thumb_get/_put`, commands `filen_cache_thumb_get`/`_put`, the latter with the JPEG as the body via `invokeWithBytes`, ≤ 512 KiB). A thumbnail is laid out like its file and named `<name>.<modified ms>-<size>.jpg`, so **a changed file simply has none until one is made for its new version, which replaces the old**. In a branch a file the branch has *not* changed shows the account's thumbnail; one it changed keeps its own in `tb`. **Committing or discarding a branch deletes both places** — its `b/MMM` pair and its `tb/MMM` pair (`delete_branch_files_and_rows`); "Clear the cache" clears the account's `t` folder, and disconnecting the account removes the account's whole `NNN` folder in `a` (content, branches and thumbnails together). (Rust unit tests: versions, replacement, branch/account separation, both deletions.)
- Not done: thumbnails aren't refreshed by the file's *content* changing with the same date and size; a Filen thumbnail is made from the file as the cache has it.

## Converting a video for compatible playback (`video_transcode.rs`)

**"Add a way to play any video file (including any .mkv file) with both video and audio… using OS installed video/audio codecs."** Discussed first, since this is a genuinely architectural choice, not a bug fix: offered three options (hand off to the OS's own player; native OS decode-and-remux in Rust/Kotlin, kept inside the app's own viewer; bundle a transcoding library like ffmpeg) and the person chose the middle one. Scoped, and said so up front, to what was actually tractable in one pass:

- **Windows desktop only.** Android would need an entirely separate MediaCodec/MediaExtractor implementation through the existing JNI bridge pattern — not attempted here, and refused with an honest "not available on this platform yet" (the same shape `secure_store.rs`'s iOS stub and `focus_admin_window`'s Android stub already use).
- **Convert once, to a cached file — not a live streaming transcode.** A real-time pipeline that keeps pace with an HTTP Range request while simultaneously decoding and encoding would mean synchronizing Media Foundation's own pull-based reads with the piece-by-piece server this app already has for every other file (`file_serving.rs`) — a much bigger, riskier design than converting once, to a file, and serving that file exactly the way every other cached file in this app already is.

**Why the browser alone can't do this.** Chromium's own `<video>` decodes with whatever codecs are compiled into *it*, not whatever the OS has — it never consults the OS's own codec registry at all, so an OS-installed codec pack changes nothing for the in-page player by itself. Windows Media Foundation, by contrast, *does* search every registered decoder on the system — including a third-party pack's own — the moment it's asked to decode a stream to a *different* type than the one it's natively in, which is the whole reason to route through it rather than trying to patch the browser's own decoding.

**The pipeline** (`platform::convert`, Windows-only, behind `#[cfg(windows)]` with an honest-error `#[cfg(not(windows))]` sibling): `MFCreateSourceReaderFromURL` opens the file. Every stream is probed by index (`GetNativeMediaType`, stopping at the first `MF_E_INVALIDSTREAMNUMBER`) to find the *first* actual video stream and the *first* actual audio stream — concrete numeric indices (`find_streams`), not the `MF_SOURCE_READER_FIRST_..._STREAM` sentinel constants, which are only trustworthy for *selecting* a stream, not for interpreting what `ReadSample`'s own `actual_stream_index` output later reports a sample belongs to. Only those two (of however many a container holds — a second audio track, subtitles) are selected; each is told to decode to an intermediate uncompressed type (`MFVideoFormat_NV12`, `MFAudioFormat_PCM`) via `SetCurrentMediaType` — this is what makes the *decoder* side format-agnostic: whatever codec the stream is actually in, Media Foundation's own topology resolver finds a decoder MFT for it the moment it's asked for a type the stream isn't already in. `MFCreateSinkWriterFromURL` then writes a new file: each stream is `AddStream`-ed with its *output* type (H.264 for video, built by `CopyAllItems`-ing the decoded type's own frame size/rate/aspect/interlace attributes and overriding just the subtype and a bitrate — avoiding ever having to pack `MF_MT_FRAME_SIZE`/`MF_MT_FRAME_RATE`'s own `UINT64` high/low halves by hand; AAC for audio, picked from `MFTranscodeGetAudioOutputAvailableTypes`'s own list of what the system's AAC encoder actually supports — its sample rate/channel count/bitrate combinations are a small, discrete set the encoder MFT accepts, not anything a caller may simply invent, unlike video's much more permissive bitrate — `pick_aac_output_type` prefers an exact match to the decoded PCM's own rate/channels, else just the first available type) and `SetInputMediaType`-ed with the *decoded* type from the reader. From there it's a plain pump: `ReadSample`/`WriteSample` in a loop (`MF_SOURCE_READER_ANY_STREAM`, dispatched to the right output stream by which concrete index each sample actually came back on) until both selected streams report end-of-stream, then `Finalize`. A source with only one of the two tracks (silent video, audio-only) is handled the same way with just that one stream added; a source with *neither* is refused outright.

**Caching is the exact same folder-pairs shape thumbnails already use** — asked for directly, once a follow-up question about where the file was actually cached ("where is the H.264/AAC re-encoded video file being cached to?") made the mismatch with the rest of the app's own caching conventions obvious, and the person said so plainly: "let's use the same approach we already use to cache files, but for re-encoded videos we'll use the 'm' folder... full folder hierarchy recreated from relative paths, separated per root and per filen account." Originally a flat, content-addressed `files/video-transcodes/` folder keyed by a SHA-256 hash of the root+path; redesigned into `files/a/NNN/m/…` — a new sibling of `c`/`b`/`t`/`tb` inside the owner's own numbered folder (`layout.rs`'s `FILES_VIDEO_TRANSCODES_FOLDER`, now `"m"`), laid out like the owner's own files and named `<name>.<modified>-<size>.mp4` (`files_cache.rs`'s `versioned_cache_file`, generalized from what used to be `thumb_file`'s own JPEG-only logic so the two share one implementation — `is_versioned_cache_file_of`/`remove_older_versioned_cache_files` the same way) — the same "a changed file simply has no converted copy until one is made for its new version" idea thumbnails already use. **Separated per root and per Filen account, as asked**: `files_cache.rs`'s `m_root`/`m_path`/`m_done`/`m_lookup` (Filen account side) and `local_branches.rs`'s `local_m_root`/`local_m_path`/`local_m_done`/`local_m_lookup` (a root of this device, via `picked_roots::root_guid_of`) are the two parallel halves — mirroring exactly how thumbnails themselves are split between the two. **Only the local half is actually wired up today**: `video_transcode.rs` still only converts local files (a Filen file isn't a real local path until the cache has it, a different problem this pass didn't take on), so the Filen-side methods are architecturally complete and Rust-tested in shape but not yet called by anything (`#[allow(dead_code)]`, with a comment pointing at why) — ready for whenever Filen source conversion is attempted, rather than needing a second redesign then. **An older cached conversion of the same file is only removed once the new one is confirmed in place** (`m_done`/`local_m_done`, called after the rename into place succeeds) — not upfront, unlike a thumbnail's own instant write: a conversion can run for minutes and can fail partway, and deleting a still-good old copy before a replacement is confirmed would leave nothing cached at all if the new attempt never finishes. Written to a `.mp4.part` file beside the final name and renamed into place only once `Finalize` succeeds — the same safety convention as everything else this app writes piece by piece. Goes with its owner the same way a thumbnail does: forgetting a root drops its conversions along with its thumbnails (`local_drop_thumbnails`, extended to also remove `m`), and disconnecting a Filen account drops its whole `NNN` folder, `m` included. Protected automatically, as before: `files/` as a whole is already a protected path (`data_location::protected_paths`).

**Serving** is `/@video-transcode/<root>/<path>` — the identical shape `/@device/<root>/<path>` already has, not a cache-internal filename exposed as a public address (`Special::VideoTranscode { root, path }` in `notes_pages.rs`, parsed from the tail the same way `/@device/` splits its own). A request re-resolves the *real* file fresh on every call (`video_transcode::lookup`: `FsScope::check_in`, re-stat'd, then the real local root's guid and `local_m_lookup`) and only then serves it through the ordinary `file_serving::respond_file_blocking` (Range support, pieces, the lot) — or 404/forbidden if the file or its conversion isn't there. **This closes a gap the first version of this feature had**: the old address carried an unguessable SHA-256 hash but no per-request re-check that the *requesting* window still had `FsScope` access to the original file — now every single request is re-validated against the caller's own file-scope access, not just the one that started the conversion, and a source file that has since changed naturally serves "not found" rather than a stale conversion. Addressed by `video_transcode_progress`'s own `url` field (`address_of(root, path)`, the same `USER_PROTOCOL`/`navigation_url` pattern `notes_pages::media_url` already uses) — built from the file's own *path*, not a cache-internal filename, so it stays stable across a redesign like this one and for an already-cached hit resolved without ever starting a new job.

**Commands** (`user-apps.json`, any window — not admin-only: this is about playing a file the window already has read access to, nothing more privileged): `video_transcode_begin(root, path)` resolves the file through the ordinary `FsScope::check_in` (so a window can only convert what it could already read), computes the cache key, and resolves at once with `done: true` if a matching cached conversion already exists; otherwise it spawns the actual conversion on a blocking task (`tauri::async_runtime::spawn_blocking`, fire-and-forget — the command itself returns a job id immediately) and returns. **Ownership matches `filen_cache.rs`'s `UploadSessions`/`SeedSessions` exactly** (`caller_key`, filtered lookup by `(id, owner)`): a job belongs to the window that began it, and another window's `video_transcode_progress` for someone else's job id is refused outright — unlike those two siblings, though, a job here keeps running in the background regardless of who later asks about it, so there's no "abandoned session" cleanup to do. **Progress is a plain ratio of the output file's own growing size against the input's** — not the actual decode position, which would need `IMFPresentationDescriptor`'s own `MF_PD_DURATION` (a `PROPVARIANT`, needing two more Cargo features this module didn't otherwise need) just to turn into a percentage; a transcoded file is usually a comparable order of magnitude to its source for similar quality settings, so this is a reasonable, honest approximation, capped at 99% until `Finalize` actually completes so it never visibly finishes early. A separate `std::thread` polls the growing `.part` file's size every 400ms while the blocking conversion task runs, writing into the job's `AtomicU32`. Two more commands, both plain and read-only, sit beside it: `video_transcode_status(root, path)` answers `{ cached, url }` without starting anything (`video_transcode::lookup`, the same one the serving route itself uses), and `video_transcode_delete(root, path)` removes the cached conversion of the file's *current* version, if any (see "Whether a video plays here as is" below).

**Frontend** (`MediaViewer.tsx`): a `Wand2`-icon button in the top bar, shown only when `item.kind === 'video'` and the file's storage is `UserFolder` or `DeviceFolder` (never `FilenCloud` — a Filen file isn't a real local path until the cache has it, a different problem this pass didn't take on). Clicking it calls `videoTranscodeBegin`, then polls `videoTranscodeProgress` every 500ms (a plain recursive `setTimeout`, not `setInterval`, so a slow poll never overlaps the next one) via a **token-based cancellation** (`transcodeToken`, a ref incremented in the same effect that resets `url`/`error` on `[item, reloads]` change) rather than a `useCallback`-returned cleanup function — `useCallback` has no cleanup mechanism the way `useEffect` does, so an earlier draft that tried returning one from inside the click handler was corrected before it shipped: the returned function was simply discarded by React, never invoked, which would have left a poll loop from an abandoned item silently still running and updating state for whatever the *new* current item happens to be. Once `done` with no error, the job's `url` directly replaces the viewer's own `url` state (the same state `mediaUrl(item.file)` normally populates) — `PlayerStage` remounts (it's keyed by `url`) and starts playing the converted file in place, no extra round trip needed. An error shows in a `.media-transcode-notice` (new CSS, a `.media-notice` variant sized for a progress bar / longer text) with a Dismiss button; the progress bar reuses the existing `.progress-bar`/`.progress-bar-fill` pair the Filen cache's own download progress already established.

**Two real bugs were found live, both through the real command/UI stack — neither reproduced by the standalone Rust test alone, which is exactly why both needed the full path exercised, not just the isolated pipeline:**

1. **"The network path was not found."** `fs_scope.rs`'s `check_in` returns `std::fs::canonicalize`'s own output, which on Windows is always extended-length (`\\?\C:\...`) — Media Foundation's own URL parser reads the leading `\\` as a UNC share and tries to resolve `?` as a server name, failing outright even though the file plainly exists. A first, standalone test of `platform::convert` never hit this because it was handed a plain, already-canonical path by hand (`CSDRIVE_TEST_VIDEO`, read via `std::env::var` with no canonicalization step) — only going through the real `video_transcode_begin` (which does route through `FsScope::check_in`) exercised the actual shape of path the feature would really receive. Fixed in `hstring_url`: a plain prefix strip (`\\?\UNC\server\share\...` back to `\\server\share\...`, `\\?\C:\...` back to `C:\...`) before building the URL, applied to both the input and output paths; Rust-tested directly (`the_extended_length_prefix_is_stripped_before_building_the_url`), confirmed live afterward (the AC3 file's real error — below — replaced the spurious path error once this landed).
2. **"The converted file couldn't be created: The specified object or value does not exist."** `MFCreateSinkWriterFromURL` infers the output *container format* (MP4, ASF, …) from the URL's own file extension when no container type is given explicitly — and the real output path is a `.mp4.part` file (the caller's own write-then-rename safety convention), whose apparent extension is `.part`, not `.mp4`. A first direct test of `convert()` never hit this either, since it wrote straight to a plain `...-test.mp4` path with no `.part` suffix at all — only going through the real `video_transcode_begin` (which always targets the `.part` file) exercised it. Fixed by stopping the file-extension guesswork entirely: `MF_TRANSCODE_CONTAINERTYPE` set to `MFTranscodeContainerType_MPEG4` on the sink writer's own attributes, regardless of what the URL's extension happens to be.

**Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP, the complete command→UI stack, not the Rust pipeline in isolation):**
- **The error path, against the person's own real file** (`Umbre.S01E01.mkv`, 2.7 GB): clicking **Convert for compatible playback** in a real Notes File Manager window correctly surfaced, end to end, "This file's audio can't be decoded: The data specified for the media type is invalid, inconsistent, or not supported by this object." — confirmed (via a diagnostic `GetGUID(&MF_MT_SUBTYPE)` read, since removed from the shipped code) that the file's real audio subtype GUID is `E06D802C-DB46-11CF-B4D1-00805F6CBBEA`, the legacy DirectShow GUID for **`MEDIASUBTYPE_DOLBY_AC3`** — directly confirming the task's own premise ("right now we can't play audio on .mkv files") and that the feature correctly identifies, and correctly refuses with a specific reason, exactly the codec gap it exists to work around when no decoder is installed.
- **The success path, against a small, locally-produced H.264 MP4 with no audio track** (a byproduct of this session's own standalone pipeline testing): clicking the same button went through a real `video_transcode_begin` → background conversion → `video_transcode_progress` polling → the viewer's own `url` swapping to the served address (then still `/@video-transcode/<hash-of-root-and-path>.<mtime>-<size>.mp4`, before the caching/addressing redesign below) with **no error notice**, and the resulting `<video>` element reported `readyState: 4` (`HAVE_ENOUGH_DATA`), `videoWidth: 1920`, `videoHeight: 1080`, `duration: 12`, and — pressed to actually play — `paused: false` with `currentTime` genuinely advancing; a screenshot confirmed real decoded picture content, not a blank player. The cache file itself was confirmed on disk afterward (a real 17.3 MB `.mp4`, correctly renamed from `.part`, no leftover partial file).
- **The pipeline's own two halves, independently, directly against `platform::convert`** (bypassing the command stack, for a fast iteration loop while getting the Media Foundation API calls right — real `cargo test --lib video_transcode::tests::real_file_conversion -- --ignored --nocapture`, `#[ignore]`d so a plain `cargo test --lib` never attempts it, env-driven via `CSDRIVE_TEST_VIDEO`): a **video-only** conversion of 300 real samples from the person's own `.mkv` (video decode/encode skipped-audio path) completed in 2.3s and produced a real, playable 17.9 MB H.264 MP4; an **audio-only** conversion of a real Windows sample file (`C:\Windows\Media\Alarm01.wav`, chosen specifically because it's a real file with a codec Windows definitely has a decoder for, letting the AAC-encode half of the pipeline — untouched by the video-only test — be exercised on its own) completed in under 100ms and produced a real, valid AAC-in-MP4 file. Together with the full-stack success test above (which exercises the *same* per-stream logic, just combined and through the real command path), this gives high confidence in the combined pipeline without needing a single test file that happens to have both a real video track and a Windows-decodable audio track, which wasn't available in this isolated instance.

**The caching/addressing redesign above re-verified live too** (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP, against the real command stack — not the Rust tests alone): `video_transcode_begin` against the person's own real `Umbre.S01E01.mkv` (2.7 GB, `root: "user"`) reproduced the identical AC3 error message as before, confirming the redesign didn't disturb the error path; a nested file (`/docs/sub/other.md`, picked purely to exercise the folder layout, not a real video) failed to open as expected but — before failing — correctly created `files/a/001/m/docs/sub/` on disk, proving the full relative-path hierarchy is recreated under `m` exactly as asked, not just a flat folder; converting a real, small, already-H.264 MP4 (`Umbre.S01E01.converted-test.mp4`) succeeded and resolved to `http://csuser.localhost/@video-transcode/user/Umbre.S01E01.converted-test.mp4` — the new `/@device/`-shaped address — with the cached file landing at `files/a/001/m/Umbre.S01E01.converted-test.mp4.<mtime>-<size>.mp4`; fetching that address directly with a real `Range` header answered `206`, `video/mp4`, the exact requested byte count, confirming the new per-request `FsScope::check_in` + cache re-lookup path actually serves real bytes, not just resolves a URL; calling `video_transcode_begin` again on the same file resolved at once (`done: true`, the same URL) with no second file written to disk, confirming the cache-hit shortcut survived the redesign. The older-conversion-is-removed-only-after-the-new-one-lands behavior (`local_m_done`) and the Filen-account side (`m_path`/`m_done`/`m_lookup`) were not separately re-exercised live here — both are exactly the same, already-Rust-tested code the thumbnail cache has used for years (`versioned_cache_file`/`remove_older_versioned_cache_files`), just parameterized differently, and the Filen half still has no real caller to exercise it against (see "Caching" above).

**Is the re-encoded file larger than the source?** Asked directly, and the real numbers say **yes, noticeably** — two compounding reasons, not one: the whole real `Umbre.S01E01.mkv` (2,925,274,205 bytes over its own measured 45:18 = 2718s duration, "Media: the viewer…" above) runs at **≈8.61 Mbps combined** (video + AC3 audio + container overhead), so the video stream alone is a bit under that; the video-only 12-second test conversion of the *same* file produced 17,881,613 bytes, **≈11.92 Mbps** — already higher than the whole source's combined rate, for video alone. `video_bitrate()`'s own pixel-area formula targets `1920×1080 × 0.1 × 30 ≈ 6.22 Mbps` for this resolution — so the encoder's *actual* output (11.92 Mbps) ran at roughly **1.9×** its own configured target, meaning Media Foundation's H.264 encoder MFT isn't tightly honoring the plain `MF_MT_AVG_BITRATE` hint this module sets (expected behavior for its default rate-control mode, which prioritizes quality over a hard size ceiling) — on top of a target that was already in the same ballpark as a well-compressed source to begin with. Net effect: a re-encoded file can easily end up 40–50%+ larger than a source that was efficiently encoded in the first place (as most real-world rips are), even though the *target* bitrate alone looked reasonable on paper. **Not fixed in this pass** — tightening this would mean setting `CODECAPI_AVEncCommonRateControlMode` (confirmed to exist in the `windows` crate: the GUID `1c0608e9-370c-4710-8a58-cb6181c42423` and `eAVEncCommonRateControlMode_CBR`/`Quality`/etc.) via `ICodecAPI` on the H.264 encoder MFT to actually enforce the configured rate, and/or lowering the per-pixel constant — a real quality/size tradeoff decision, not a bug fix, so it's left for the person to weigh in on rather than changed unprompted.

**Whether a video plays here as is, or needs converting first — and whether it's already been converted** (asked for directly, in the same conversation as the file-size question above: "for video files add information about whether its playable by our app as is, or if it requires re-encoding first... for already re-encoded videos, add an option to delete the re-encoded file"). The backend can answer the first question from the file's own real codecs, without running `convert` at all: `video_playability(root, path)` (Windows only, the same scope `video_transcode_begin` already has) opens the source reader the same way `convert` does but stops at `GetNativeMediaType`, reads each selected stream's `MF_MT_SUBTYPE` and classifies it (`platform::codec_info`: a short, **conservative** allowlist of codecs this app's own webview reliably plays — H.264, VP8, VP9, AV1 for video; AAC, MP3, Opus, Vorbis, FLAC for audio — everything else, HEVC included, is answered `false` rather than guessed at, since HEVC's real-world browser support is exactly the kind of inconsistent, OS-codec-dependent situation this whole feature exists to work around for other codecs) into `{ videoCodec, audioCodec, playable }` — `None` for a track the file doesn't have, `playable: false` for a file with neither track found or any unrecognized one. "Already converted" is a separate question, answered by the `video_transcode_status`/`video_transcode_delete` pair above.

**`MediaViewer.tsx`** calls both `video_playability` and `video_transcode_status` whenever it shows a transcodable item (the same `transcodeRoot` gate the Convert button already uses), against the file's *real* source address — a new `sourceUrl` state, kept apart from the viewer's own `url` (which becomes the converted copy's own address once a conversion succeeds), so the playability check always reads the original file, never a conversion already loaded in the viewer. A small text line under the top bar reports **"This video plays in this app as is."** or **"This video needs conversion to play here (video: …, audio: …)."**, with **"A converted copy is already cached."** beneath it once one exists; a `Trash2`-icon button, **Delete the converted copy**, sits in the top bar next to Convert only while a cached conversion exists, and deleting it (`videoTranscodeDelete`) clears the "already cached" line at once and, if the viewer was currently playing the deleted copy, falls back to the real source address — which re-triggers the playability check, correctly reporting "needs conversion" again since the cached copy is genuinely gone.

**The Help page's own codec-installation instructions** — the other half of the same ask ("in the help page add user instructions about how to install audio/video codecs on Android and Windows") — are `docs/installing-audio-video-codecs.md`, embedded and rendered the identical way `docs/keyboard-shortcuts.md` already is (`help_docs::get_codec_help_html`, admin-only, `markdown::render_fragment`, one document serving both a person reading the repo and the in-app Help tab — `without_leading_heading` reused verbatim) — a new section, **"Installing audio/video codecs"**, added to `HelpTab.tsx`'s own `SECTIONS` list (so it shows in the table of contents) and fetched the same way the keyboard-shortcuts section already is. **Deliberately doesn't name a specific third-party codec pack** — recommending a specific download isn't something this doc can respons­ibly vouch for sight-unseen, so it instead explains what a codec pack *does* (registers decoders with Media Foundation, the same mechanism `video_transcode.rs` searches), points at Microsoft's own Media Feature Pack for N/KN Windows editions specifically, and gives general cautionary advice (prefer independently-reviewed options, decline bundled toolbars). **Android's section is honest about there being no real fix today**: codec support there is baked into the device's own firmware, not something a user installs the way a Windows codec pack works, and the app's own conversion feature isn't on Android yet either — the one thing that reliably works is converting on a Windows machine first, then copying the result over.

Rust-tested (`video_transcode.rs`): the served address is the same `/@device/`-shaped form for a given root+path, every time, never a cache-internal name (`the_served_address_is_the_same_shape_device_paths_already_have` — replacing the pre-redesign `cache_paths_are_unique_…`/`only_this_modules_own_cache_file_shape_…`, which tested the now-removed flat hash scheme); the extended-length path prefix is stripped correctly in both the `C:\...` and the `\\server\share\...` UNC cases, and an already-plain path is left alone (`the_extended_length_prefix_is_stripped_before_building_the_url`); `codec_info` names every known codec and its playability correctly — H.264/AAC playable, the exact real GUID this project found live for the person's own `.mkv` (`MFAudioFormat_Dolby_AC3`) answered AC3/not-playable, HEVC deliberately not trusted despite being a real video codec, and an unrecognized GUID always answered not-playable, never guessed (`codec_info_names_known_codecs_and_is_conservative_about_the_rest`). `files_cache/local_branches.rs`: converted videos are cached exactly like thumbnails — nothing until one is made, a changed version gets its own entry, the older one is removed only once the new one is confirmed in place, deleting the current version's entry (`local_m_delete`) touches nothing else and is a no-op when there's nothing cached, and forgetting the root drops whatever's left too (`converted_videos_are_cached_like_thumbnails_and_go_with_the_root`, the `local_m_path`/`local_m_done`/`local_m_lookup`/`local_m_delete` counterpart of the existing `thumbnails_of_a_branch_are_kept_apart_and_go_with_it`). `help_docs.rs`: the codec-help document starts with a heading that's correctly dropped before rendering, and the real Windows/Android guidance text survives rendering (`the_codec_help_document_starts_with_a_heading_and_survives_rendering`). `notes_pages.rs`: `/@video-transcode/<root>/<path>` parses the same root/path shape `/@device/` already does, root/path required and non-empty (`the_reserved_address_space_is_read_strictly`, extended).

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP): the Help tab's new section renders with real formatted markdown (headings, bold, a bullet list) under its own table-of-contents entry; a pre-existing, unrelated horizontal-scroll in the Help tab (from the `example-toolbar` sample's own long, unwrapped HTML/JS `CodeBlock`) was confirmed via direct element measurement to be unrelated to the new section, not a regression it introduced. **The playability/status/delete trio re-verified separately, through the real command stack and the real UI, not just the Rust tests**: `video_playability` against the person's own real `Umbre.S01E01.mkv` correctly answered `{videoCodec: "H.264", audioCodec: "AC3 (Dolby Digital)", playable: false}`; against a small already-H.264 test file, `{videoCodec: "H.264", audioCodec: null, playable: true}`; `video_transcode_status`/`video_transcode_delete` correctly reported, and then removed, a real cached conversion — confirmed both via the backend (`cached: false` afterward, the file genuinely gone from `files/a/001/m/` on disk) and through the actual UI: opening the small test file in a real Notes File Manager window (`open_new_secondary_window`, a real row click to open the viewer) showed "This video plays in this app as is."; converting it first and reopening showed "A converted copy is already cached." with a real `Trash2` **Delete the converted copy** button beside Convert; clicking it (a genuine CDP mouse click at the button's measured screen position, not `.click()`) removed the button, dropped the "already cached" line, and the backend confirmed the cache entry was gone — a screenshot confirmed the on-screen layout matches (the info line and both icon buttons sit cleanly in the top bar with no overlap). `npm run build`, `cargo build` and `cargo test --lib` (276 tests, 1 ignored) all clean throughout.
