Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP keystrokes via `Input.insertText`/`Input.dispatchKeyEvent`, a Notes File Manager editor): scrolled to the bottom of a 60-line file, caret at the very end, six real alternating `Input.insertText`/Enter rounds — before the fix, settled at `scrollTop 655` against a required `675` (20px short, content cut off); after the fix, settled at exactly `scrollTop 675` for a `675 + 648 = 1323` total, matching `scrollHeight` precisely. A control case (scrolled to the *top*, caret near the *start*, same kind of edit) confirmed the gate: `scrollTop` stayed at `0` throughout, unmoved. `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npm run build`, `cargo build` and `cargo test --lib` (276 tests, untouched — all four fixes in this section are pure frontend) all clean.

## Debouncing the per-keystroke cost of highlighting and the gutter, on a genuinely large file

**"Continuously recalculating the entire highlighting of the file can be fine on desktop but on mobile I'm worried
it might drain the battery"** — discussed first, since the person's own proposed fix (detect in advance, per
keystroke, whether a re-scan is even *needed* — "surrounded by letters" being safe, a keyword boundary or a
string/comment terminator not) was a real architectural choice, not a bug fix. **Turned down**: whether a word is
a keyword depends on the *whole word after the edit*, not the inserted character in isolation (`i`→`if`, `if`→`ifx`),
and a string/comment terminator's own escaping rules differ per language's scanner — getting this exhaustively
right would mean mirroring every highlighter's own keyword/escape/boundary logic a second time, in a second,
approximate form, and a wrong "safe to skip" guess produces a silently stuck wrong colour nothing afterward
self-corrects — a worse failure mode than the Worker pass's own always-eventually-correct one. **Debouncing the
*dispatch* to the Worker instead** (`EXACT_DISPATCH_DEBOUNCE_MS`, 200ms, `CodeEditor.tsx`'s `useBackgroundHighlighted`)
needs none of that: the instant, approximate stand-in already makes every keystroke feel immediate regardless of
when the exact pass actually runs, so delaying *only* the dispatch — reset on every further keystroke, fired once
typing actually pauses — cuts how often the whole dispatch → Worker → reply → reparse cycle runs during a
sustained burst, with no new correctness surface: a person who never pauses simply keeps seeing the (already
correct, for the overwhelmingly common case) approximate colouring until they do.

**Verifying it against a real file turned up a second, bigger problem the debounce alone didn't touch.** Tested
against the person's own real 191 KB, 2,808-line markdown note (hard-linked into an isolated debug data folder's
`user/` for the session, never copied into or touched in the person's own real notebook — confirmed via `git
status` on that notebook's repo before and after). A CPU profile (`Profiler.start`/`Profiler.stop` over CDP) of an
11-keystroke burst found `getClientRects` — not anything to do with highlighting at all — as by far the single
hottest function, at ~80ms *per keystroke* on this file: the line-number gutter's wrapped-row measurement
(`lib/wrappedLineRows.ts`'s `computeRowsPerLine`, one `Range.getClientRects()` call per source line) was deferred
by only one macrotask (`setTimeout(fn, 0)`), not actually debounced — on a file with long lines (few lines, 2,808,
well under the existing `MAX_LINES_FOR_WRAP_MEASUREMENT` safety net, but each one expensive to measure), this ran
to completion on *every* settled keystroke during continuous typing, identically whether the experimental editor
was on or off (the gutter is shared by both). **Fixed the same way**: `WRAP_MEASURE_DEBOUNCE_MS` (250ms) — the
measurement's own `setTimeout` is now reset on every keystroke instead of firing after a fixed one macrotask, so
a sustained burst pays this cost once, after it stops, not once per keystroke; the gutter simply keeps its
last-known spacing until then (the same "an approximate is fine for a moment, it self-corrects" trade already
used everywhere else in this file). Re-profiled identically afterward: `getClientRects`' total dropped from
861ms to 323ms across the same burst (≈62% less) — a real, measured improvement, not assumed from the code read
alone. A control profile of 3 idle seconds with the field still focused but *no* typing showed essentially zero
cost (3000ms idle, 17.6ms overhead, nothing else) — confirming both costs are genuinely keystroke-triggered, not
a fixed background tax this fix could never have touched.

**A third, real inefficiency was found along the way, unrelated to either debounce**: `useBackgroundHighlighted`'s
approximate branch (`tokenClassAtOffset` scanning up to the caret, then `escapeHtml`/`markWhitespace` scanning the
*entire* value) ran **unconditionally on every keystroke, in both editor modes** — even though in experimental mode
its result (`html` state) is never rendered at all (`CodeEditor`'s JSX gives the experimental `<pre>` no
`dangerouslySetInnerHTML`; only `RunPatchController`'s own, already-cheaper, localised approximate patch is), which
is exactly why toggling the experimental editor on and off made no measurable difference on this file before this
fix — the patcher's own cheaper work was paying for the old, wasteful computation too, on top of its own. **Fixed**
by reordering `CodeEditor`'s own hooks so `experimental` is known before `useBackgroundHighlighted` is called, and
skipping the expensive branch entirely when it's on — `html` is set to a cheap, merely-identity-changing counter
instead (all the scroll-to-bottom effect below it needs: that an approximate *or* exact pass just landed, never
its content). The default editor's own cost here is unchanged and unavoidable without replacing its rendering with
the same token-diff approach (not asked for) — it actually renders this string, so nothing here was wasted work
for it.

**A real, separate cost was found and *not* fixed, flagged rather than silently chased or silently dropped**: the
same CPU profile's next two hottest entries (after the wrap-measurement fix, roughly 600ms and 550ms respectively
across the same burst) are `TextFieldMenu.tsx`'s own continuous `requestAnimationFrame` loop that keeps the
floating "…" clipboard-menu trigger positioned at a focused field's corner "wherever the page scrolls or resizes
it to" — a generic mechanism every text field in the whole app shares, not something specific to `CodeEditor` or
to a big file, and confirmed (by the same idle-profile control above) to cost nothing while nothing is typed —
its own per-frame `getBoundingClientRect()` apparently becomes measurably more expensive once the adjacent
53K+-node `<pre>` has made the page's layout this heavy, piggybacking on the same reflow the fixes above already
address, but widening today's scope to a shared, app-wide component used everywhere else too wasn't done without
checking first.

Verified live throughout (isolated debug data folder — `-debug`, see "Folder layout" — never the person's real
data; the real test file was a hard link, read-only for this purpose, confirmed untouched by `git status`
afterward): `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npm run build` clean after each change; the CPU-profile comparisons above, run
against the same file reopened fresh each time (discarding any unsaved in-memory edits from a previous test run,
since nothing was ever saved to disk) so every comparison started from the identical 191,591-character content;
a fixed-wall-clock-cadence typing simulation (each character's `execCommand('insertText', …)` scheduled at its
own independent `i * 45ms` timer from the start, rather than sequentially awaited — the latter silently
serializes every character's own full reaction chain before the next one can even begin, which doesn't represent
how a real keyboard delivers keystrokes at a fixed pace regardless of how busy the app is) confirmed all
characters still land correctly, in order, with no data loss, even while visibly falling behind schedule during
the heaviest moments — the same honest trade this file's own "Editor performance" section already documents
elsewhere (a person who never pauses sees the approximate colouring throughout, catching up once they do) rather
than a claim that this one real file now has zero lag. `cargo test --lib` (276 tests, untouched — every change in
this section is pure frontend) clean throughout.

## Two more editor bugs: a stuck caret after undo/redo, and the line-number gutter's own full-file remeasure cost

**"Go to the end of a file, add a few lines quickly, then hit Undo... then Redo, the lines do appear back, but I can no longer edit them, the caret won't move past the position where those new lines appear."** Root cause, found by reading the effect ordering rather than by a lucky guess: `fit()` (`CodeEditor.tsx`, sizes the real `<textarea>` to its own `scrollHeight` so the outer frame, not the box itself, does the scrolling) reads that `scrollHeight` straight off the **real DOM element's own current content** — which for an *ordinary keystroke* is already correct the instant `fit()` runs (the browser already wrote the new character into the real, uncontrolled textarea natively, synchronously, well before any deferred `onChange` or render ever fires), but for a **programmatic** edit (undo, redo, Tab, "Insert a path…", an external reset) there is no such prior native write at all — `value` changes purely in React state, and the *separate* effect that forces the real DOM text to match it (`input.value = value`) was declared **after** `fit`'s own effect in the source, so React — which runs a component's `useLayoutEffect`s in declaration order — ran `fit()` first, measuring `scrollHeight` against the *previous* step's stale text, and only wrote the real (correct) text in afterward. A single-line Tab/Insert-a-path edit never changes the box's own required height enough for this staleness to matter (which is why those were tested extensively elsewhere in this file with no sign of it); an undo/redo that adds or removes several lines does, leaving the real `<textarea>` genuinely shorter than its own actual content, with that content silently clipped under the browser's own default overflow for a box in that state — exactly matching "the caret won't move past" the point where the newly-restored text (now unreachable to a click, invisible past the too-short box) begins. **Fixed by reordering, not rewriting**: the DOM-sync effect is now declared *before* `fit`'s own, so `fit()` always measures the real, already-synced text — the three effects' relative order is now sync → `fit` → the existing "keep the frame scrolled to its own bottom" effect, which already documented (and still correctly keeps) its own dependency on `fit` having run first.

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP, a real 5,000-line file): at the file's own end, adding three real lines then Undo then Redo — the real textarea's own CSS `height` and `scrollHeight` were read directly at every step and matched exactly each time (97536px for the un-edited state, 97614px for the edited/redone state, on both sides of the undo/redo round trip — never mismatched, which is the literal mechanism the bug lived in); typing further text immediately after Redo landed correctly at the real end of the file; placing the caret (`setSelectionRange`, the same DOM state a real click resolves to) inside the just-redone text and typing there inserted correctly at that exact position, confirmed byte-for-byte against the file's own content afterward. **One avenue not pursued further**: a literal `ArrowDown`-past-the-restored-lines reproduction doesn't apply when the edit is at the file's own literal end (there is nothing below the last line to move to, by definition) — the fix is verified instead at its actual mechanism (the height measurement itself, directly), which is what the reported symptom traces back to.

**"Every time a new line is added, all line numbers disappear for a few seconds then show again correctly"** — on a file with many thousands of lines. `computeRowsPerLine` (`lib/wrappedLineRows.ts`) already correctly measures where each line wraps, but its own cost is one `Range.getClientRects()` call *per source line* — fine for a short file, but strictly `O(the whole file)` on every single edit, however small, since the existing `WRAP_MEASURE_DEBOUNCE_MS` debounce only controls *how often* a remeasure runs during a burst, not what each one itself costs. On a file with many thousands of lines, one full remeasure can itself be a multi-hundred-millisecond (or, as reported, multi-second) synchronous block of the main thread — during which the *real* textarea already shows the new text (an ordinary keystroke's own native DOM insertion needs no React render at all) while the gutter — a plain React-rendered list, one `<span>` per line — stays frozen on whatever it showed a moment ago, visibly out of step with the text beside it; "disappear" is that mismatch, not a literal empty gutter (the list always keeps exactly `lineCount` entries, updated with `value` in the very same render).

**Fixed with `computeRowsPerLineIncremental`** (`lib/wrappedLineRows.ts`, new): a line's own row count depends only on its own text and the box's own width, never on where it sits in the document — so a line that didn't change between the last measurement and now keeps exactly the row count it already had, and only the genuinely changed run of lines (on an ordinary edit, almost always just the one being typed in) is ever measured again. `diffLines(oldLines, newLines)` — the line-array counterpart of `lib/highlightTokens.ts`'s own `diffRuns` prefix/suffix trim, the same idea already proven for the experimental editor's token patching — finds how many lines at the start and the end are untouched; everything between is the one thing actually re-measured, via the identical per-line `Range`-measurement `computeRowsPerLine` already uses (including its own established fix for a collapsed-range's degenerate zero-rect — the incremental version's one new wrinkle, the "next row" boundary needed for the changed run's own last line when there's an unchanged suffix following it, is read from the *same* non-collapsed range already built for that line's own row-start — one more `rects[rects.length-1]` read, not a second, separate measurement at the exact kind of position a collapsed range fails at). `CodeEditor.tsx`'s `measureWrappedRows` takes this path whenever a previous measurement exists at the same `lineHeightPx`; the one case it's deliberately **not** used — a frame width change, which can move where *every* line wraps, unchanged text included — still forces the original, full `computeRowsPerLine` (`measureWrappedRows(true)`, called only from the `ResizeObserver`'s own width-change branch).

Node-tested (`diffLines`, pure, no DOM — an ad-hoc script matching this project's own "Node-tested" pattern for a module with no committed test file yet): a line appended at the end, inserted at the start, inserted/edited/deleted in the middle, everything changed, and — at scale — a 50,000-line array with only its last or only one middle line changed, confirming the prefix/suffix trim finds the minimal changed run correctly and cheaply even on a huge array. **Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop, real CDP)**: a CPU profile of a real line insertion into a genuine 5,000-line file measured `getClientRects` at **22.3ms** for the incremental path, against **141.3ms** for a full remeasure of the identical file forced immediately afterward (the ResizeObserver's own width-change path, still exercising the *original*, unoptimized cost directly, same file, same session) — roughly a 6× reduction on a file of this size alone, with the gap only growing on a bigger one, since the incremental cost stays flat regardless of file length while the full one scales with it. **Correctness checked byte-exactly, not just assumed from the algorithm**: inserting a line at the very start of a 400-line file with real wrapping lines mixed in, then saving and reopening it fresh (a genuine new mount always does a full remeasure, with no shortcuts) and diffing all 402 gutter pixel positions between the incremental, in-memory result and the independent full-remeasure one — byte-for-byte identical; repeated with a genuinely different, multi-line, middle-of-file edit (replacing two lines with three different ones, net +1 line, a real prefix *and* suffix on both sides of the changed run) — byte-for-byte identical again. (A first attempt at this same comparison, forcing a full remeasure in place via a CSS inline-width trick rather than a real save-and-reopen, produced a real-looking mismatch that turned out to be a `box-sizing` artifact of that trick itself, not a bug in the fix — caught by independently measuring the exact same line's own wrap behavior with a plain, hand-built `Range` outside the app's own code, which showed the *trick's* measurement was the wrong one, not the incremental result; recorded here so the same dead end isn't walked into again.) `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npm run build` and `cargo test --lib` (277 tests, untouched — both fixes in this section are pure frontend) all clean.

**Reported live again, right after shipping the fix above: "it's slower now after the fix for the line numbers (and it's slower when I disable line numbers altogether too). It's just as slow as the default editor."** Two separate, real problems, not one:

1. **The gutter's own measurement ran unconditionally, regardless of `editor.lineNumbers`.** `measureWrappedRows` only ever checked `settings?.wrapLines === false` — nothing checked whether the gutter was even being *drawn*, so turning line numbers off (which only hides the `<span>`s `gutterRowStarts` feeds — the one and only consumer of `wrapMeasure`) left every edit still paying for the full measurement pass, `diffLines` and all, for a result nobody was going to see. Fixed with a second early return, `if (settings?.lineNumbers === false) return`, and `settings?.lineNumbers` added to the scheduling effect's own dependency array so turning the gutter back *on* mid-session schedules a real measurement at once rather than leaving `wrapMeasure` stuck on a stale value.
2. **`diffLines`'s own line-array approach added back real, whole-file-sized overhead on *every* debounced remeasure, regardless of the edit's own position** — `oldLines = previousValue.split('\n')` and `newLines = value.split('\n')`, each allocating one substring per line, followed by a line-by-line `===` walk from both ends. On a big file this is a real, measurable cost paid every single time, eating most of what reusing unchanged lines' row counts was meant to save — together with gap 1 above, enough to leave the "optimization" no faster than simply remeasuring everything, exactly matching "just as slow as the default editor" (the gutter's own cost is shared by both rendering paths, so once it dominates, whatever advantage the experimental editor has elsewhere stops showing). **Fixed by finding the common prefix/suffix of the two raw *strings* instead** (`previousValue.charCodeAt(i) === value.charCodeAt(i)`, from both ends) — no allocation at all, and the same or better cost for the common, localized-edit case. A line only counts as unchanged once its *entire* span, trailing `\n` included, falls inside the matched region — found via `countLeadingUnchangedLines`/`countTrailingUnchangedLines`, each a binary search over `lineStartOffsets` rather than a scan. `diffLines` itself is gone, superseded.

**Three genuine correctness gaps in this replacement were caught before any of it ever reached a browser** — not by inspection, but by a dedicated Node stress test (`countLeadingUnchangedLines`/`countTrailingUnchangedLines`'s own doc comments in `lib/wrappedLineRows.ts` have the full technical account) comparing, over 100,000+ randomized edits, how many leading/trailing lines the new character-level approach trusted against what the old, slower, already-proven-correct `diffLines` would have trusted — flagging only the *unsafe* direction (trusting *more* than the reference would have; trusting *fewer* is just a harmless missed optimization, confirmed separately as always safe by a second, end-to-end simulation that reconstructed the full row array both ways and diffed them, 50,000 more cases, zero mismatches):
1. A line can be validated by `countLeadingUnchangedLines` using `value.length` as its own end (the file's literal last line) with **no real `\n` behind that confirmation at all** — `previousValue` can have more content past the matched prefix that makes its own line at that index a *different* line than `value`'s (possibly truncated) last one (`previousValue = "ab\ncd"`, `value = "ab\n"`: the 3-character prefix covers all of `value`, but `previousRows`'s line 1 is `"cd"`'s row count, not an empty line's). Fixed by also requiring `previousValue.length === prefixLen` whenever the leading count would otherwise reach every line.
2. The suffix's mirror image: the outermost trailing line counted as unchanged is vulnerable the same way, but only when its own start lands *exactly* on the matched suffix's edge (an interior boundary is always safe, proven by the matched region's content being identical on both sides). Fixed by checking that `previousValue.length - suffixLen` is itself `0` or follows a `\n` in `previousValue`.
3. `value`'s own trailing degenerate line (when it ends with `\n`, or is empty) **trivially satisfies the suffix check for any `suffixLen`, including `0`** — a wholly vacuous match confirming zero real characters — and because it's always the outermost line the moment the raw count is `>= 1`, capping the count (gap 1's fix) doesn't exclude it; it has to be subtracted unconditionally whenever `value` ends degenerate, before the edge check in gap 2 ever runs.

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop): `npx tsc --noEmit` clean after both fixes. The Node stress test (ad-hoc, matching this project's own pattern for a module with no committed test file yet) is the load-bearing verification for the three correctness gaps — a live CDP re-profile of the original 22.3ms-vs-141.3ms comparison wasn't repeated for this follow-up, since nothing about the *measurement cost itself* changed (only correctness and the unrelated `lineNumbers` gate did); the stress test's own 100,000+ cases (plus 50,000 end-to-end row-array reconstructions) are what give confidence the replacement is both safe and, for the realistic localized-edit case, allocation-free where `diffLines` wasn't.

## The experimental editor is no longer experimental — it's the only one

**"Make the experimental editor the only editor for our app (so remove that checkbox from the settings page)."**
Once the token-patching renderer (`lib/runPatchController.ts`) had been live for a while with no measured
regression and the flicker it was built to remove gone, keeping the older, simpler full-`dangerouslySetInnerHTML`
path around — reachable only through an opt-in Settings checkbox, off by default — had nothing left to earn its
own continued existence as a second code path to keep correct. Removed outright rather than left as dead code
behind a setting nobody could reach any more:

- **`editorSettings.ts`**: `experimentalEditor` dropped from `EditorSettings`, `DEFAULTS` and `KEYS` entirely — a
  stored `editor.experimentalEditor` value from before this change is simply never read again (the generic
  `global_settings` table needs no migration for an orphaned key).
- **`SettingsTab.tsx`**: the checkbox and its row removed; `setEditorFlag`'s own key union no longer names it.
- **`CodeEditor.tsx`**: `useBackgroundHighlighted` lost its `experimental` parameter and the `caretOffset` one it
  only needed for the removed branch — it now unconditionally does what the experimental path always did (a
  cheap, identity-only placeholder for `html`, since nothing has rendered that string directly since
  `RunPatchController` took over); the two `RunPatchController` effects lost their `if (!experimental) return`
  guards and the `experimental ? highlightRef.current : null` ternary, always attaching and always patching now;
  the render itself lost the `experimental ? (...) : (...)` branch — one `<pre>`, with no
  `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` prop, replaces the two differently-keyed elements that used to let React remount
  cleanly across a mode switch that can no longer happen.
- **`runPatchController.ts`**/**`highlightTokens.ts`**: doc comments that described "the experimental path's
  counterpart to the default path's…" rewritten to describe the one mechanism directly, with a short historical
  pointer back to `CodeEditor.tsx`'s own module doc rather than repeating the whole story in each place.

Verified live (isolated data folder, desktop): `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npm run build` both clean. **Not
re-exercised live in this pass** — the change is purely subtractive (deleting the now-unreachable branch and
its guard conditions, not introducing new logic), and the one remaining path is exactly the experimental
renderer this file's own history already live-verified extensively (undo/redo, Tab, Page Up/Down, the
dropped-keystroke fix, the gutter's wrapped-row measurement, all of it) before this change ever touched it.

## Virtualizing the editor: only the visible window (plus buffer) is ever real DOM

**"Only generate the markup that is currently visible (plus 3 or so screens before and after the current
screen) and only generate additional markup when needed (the user scrolls, or navigates with the keyboard
in the editor). This should affect both the code itself and the line numbers."** The prior architecture
(above) already made the DOM *patching* cheap and localised — but every full rebuild (a freshly opened
file, a width change, a window-shift) still built the overlay's and the gutter's DOM for the **whole file**,
and a file's own node count (one per token, one more per whitespace character) was flagged as a known,
unaddressed cost on a very large file ("The experimental, token-patching code editor" above: "this bookkeeping
cost scales with file size"). Discussed with the person first, since this changes the architecture, not a
bug: two forks were settled before any code — **unvisited regions of a wrapped file use an estimate (one row
per line) until actually scrolled into view and measured, self-correcting from there** (the same trade-off
every virtualized variable-height list makes), and **it applies unconditionally, to every file size, with no
line-count threshold** — a small file's own window simply ends up covering the whole thing, which doubles as
this feature's own built-in regression check.

**Tokenisation stays whole-document; only *materialising* it into DOM is windowed.** `highlight()`, the
Worker (`highlightWorker.ts`) and `HighlightScheduler` are untouched — investigated directly before deciding
this: the Markdown and HTML scanners carry state across lines (an open fence, an open `<!--` comment, a raw
`<script>`/`<style>` body found by a forward search), so re-tokenising only a visible slice would mean first
reconstructing that state from everything before it — re-deriving most of the cost windowing exists to
remove, for a cost that was never the measured bottleneck anyway (it already runs off the main thread).
`lib/highlightTokens.ts`'s `tokensOf` still parses the **whole** exact html into one `Run[]` list — cheap, a
plain string scan, not DOM — and `CodeEditor.tsx` memoises it once per exact update
(`fullExactRuns = useMemo(() => tokensOf(exact.html), [exact])`). Only a **slice** of that list, cut with the
already-existing `splitRunsAt`, is ever turned into real nodes.

**`windowRange` (`{startLine, endLine}`, end-exclusive)** is state, recomputed by a new, pure,
binary-search-based `computeWindowRange(scrollTop, clientHeight, lineHeightPx, starts, bufferScreens)`
(`lib/wrappedLineRows.ts`) — `WINDOW_BUFFER_SCREENS = 3`, per the ask. Three things move it:
- **The initial estimate, corrected once mounted.** The very first render can't know the frame's real size
  yet, so it guesses one screen's worth from `window.innerHeight` — a huge file's first paint still goes
  through the same windowed path as every later one, never the whole, unbounded tree this feature exists to
  avoid — and a dedicated **mount-only** `useLayoutEffect` corrects it to the frame's own real `clientHeight`
  immediately, before paint. Deliberately its *own* effect, not folded into the existing `ResizeObserver`
  callback below — see why, two paragraphs down.
- **Scrolling**, via `handleScroll`'s own hysteresis check (cheap arithmetic, no DOM, run unthrottled): only
  once the *unbuffered* visible range gets within one screen of the window's own edge — the buffer being
  eaten into — is a fresh, fully-rebuffered window computed and adopted. This is what keeps a one-pixel
  scroll from rebuilding a many-hundred-line window on every tick; right after adopting one, the margin on
  both sides is a full `WINDOW_BUFFER_SCREENS` again.
- **The caret moving outside the current window** (typing, undo/redo, "Insert a path…", anything) —
  recentred **during render itself**, the official React "adjust state during render" pattern: a ref tracks
  the last `value` seen, and the moment it's changed, the caret's line is checked against `windowRange`; if
  outside, `setWindowRange(...)` is called synchronously inside the render, which makes React discard this
  render's output and immediately re-render with the corrected window, before anything commits or an effect
  runs — no extra visible frame. Reads `restoreCaret.current` (the *intended* caret of a still-in-flight
  programmatic edit) in preference to the DOM's own `selectionEnd`, which for a programmatic edit hasn't been
  written yet at this point in the render.

**A window move forces a clean rebuild; an edit *within* a stable window still uses the existing minimal-diff
patching.** `RunPatchController` gained one new public method, `resetLiveState()` (the three lines
`attachContainer` already had, factored out so they're callable without the container element itself
changing) — called whenever `windowRange` has changed since the controller's own last apply (tracked via a
shared `lastAppliedWindowRef`, checked and updated by whichever of the two patcher effects runs first in a
commit, so the reset never happens twice for one window change). Diffing the old window's slice against a
*differently-positioned* new one would find almost nothing in common anyway (the two are offset, not merely
appended-to) — there's no reason to try, and rebuilding is cheap precisely because a window is bounded.

**`applyApproximate`'s own basis is deliberately asymmetric** — `windowValue` (what to render) is sliced to
the window, but `exactRuns`/`caretOffset` are kept **document-absolute, unsliced**. This was caught before
it shipped, not after: the first draft sliced both: `splitRunsAt` is itself an `O(whole document)` walk, and
slicing it on *every keystroke* (the approximate path runs on every one) would have reintroduced the exact
class of per-keystroke, whole-file cost this same session already found and fixed once, for a different
function (`diffLines`'s own O(file) allocation, in "Debouncing the per-keystroke cost..." above). Keeping
them unsliced is safe because `classAtOffset(exactRuns, caretOffset)` only needs internal consistency
between the two (both already share the document's own basis), never a comparison against the window-relative
`newValue` directly — slicing is only genuinely unavoidable in `applyExact`, which runs on the *debounced*
exact reply, not every keystroke.

**The wrapped-row array (`wrapMeasure.rows`) became "best known so far," not "fully measured or nothing."**
`computeRowsPerLineIncremental` — the whole-document incremental measurer built for the earlier gutter-perf
fix — was **deleted outright**, not adapted: its entire premise (that `highlightEl` holds the *whole*
document, so any changed line, anywhere, can be found and remeasured in place) is incompatible with a
windowed `highlightEl`, which now only ever contains the current window's own text. Its DOM-free
prefix/suffix logic — all three correctness-gap fixes from that earlier investigation, preserved verbatim —
was extracted into a new, pure, exported `carryForwardRows(previousValue, previousRows, value)`
(`lib/wrappedLineRows.ts`): given the *old*, best-known row-count array and the old/new text, it returns a
full-length array where the proven-unchanged prefix/suffix lines keep their old counts and the changed middle
defaults to `1` — no DOM, no incremental *measurement* at all, just correctly *shifting* indices across an
edit that inserts or deletes lines. `measureWrappedRows` (`CodeEditor.tsx`) now: calls `carryForwardRows` (or
starts an all-`1` array, on a width change or a brand-new file) to get the full-length baseline, then always
runs a **fresh, full** `computeRowsPerLine` (unchanged — it was never the whole-document version that had an
incremental cost, only the *measurement* did) on just the current window's own text, and splices that
result in at the window's absolute line offsets. A window is small and bounded by construction, so there's
no longer a reason for an incremental-*within-the-window* variant — a fresh measurement of a few hundred
lines costs nothing like a fresh measurement of fifty thousand did.

**A real bug was found live, not by inspection, in the pre-existing (not newly written) `ResizeObserver`
effect** — mount-only (`[]` deps, so the observer itself isn't endlessly resubscribed), its callback's own
closure over `measureWrappedRows` was already known to be frozen at mount, a narrow, acknowledged, deliberately-
left-alone gap *before* this session (a resize after an edit would remeasure against the original `value`).
Virtualization made the exact same staleness substantially worse without anyone having to introduce a new
bug: the frozen closure also freezes `windowRange`/`windowStartChar`/`windowEndChar`/`wrapMeasure` at mount,
so a resize **after the window has ever moved** writes freshly-measured rows at the *wrong* absolute line
indices, or slices a `windowText` against content `highlightEl` no longer holds. Caught live on a 5,000-line
file: scrolled far from its mount-time window, then narrowed the editor frame — the content visibly wrapped
(confirmed directly with a hand-built `Range.getClientRects()` check across the wrapped line's own text
nodes, independent of the gutter's own math) while the gutter kept showing flat, unwrapped one-row-per-line
spacing, silently stale. **Fixed with a ref, not by touching the observer's own subscribe/unsubscribe
lifecycle** (deliberately left mount-only, exactly as the pre-existing design already had it, to avoid
resubscribing a `ResizeObserver` on every render for no reason): `measureWrappedRowsRef.current =
measureWrappedRows` is set in the render body on every render (a plain, synchronous mutation, not an effect —
safe since refs don't trigger re-renders), and the `ResizeObserver` callback calls
`measureWrappedRowsRef.current(true)` instead of the function it closed over at mount. This is a genuine,
in-scope fix — it's a correctness regression this very feature introduced into existing code being actively
touched, not the same narrow pre-existing gap being left alone a second time.

**One deliberately-chosen test-methodology trap, recorded so it isn't walked into again**: dispatching many
synthetic `PageDown` `keydown` events back-to-back, in one synchronous script loop with no `await` between
them, does **not** replicate a real, rapid keypress burst — `CodeEditor.tsx`'s own Page Up/Down handler reads
`e.currentTarget.selectionStart`/`selectionEnd` *synchronously* at dispatch time but applies the move inside a
deferred `setTimeout(..., 0)` (the same "defer the handling, not the shortcut detection" pattern this file's
own "Editor performance" section already documents). Firing all of them before any deferred callback runs
means every one reads the *same*, stale starting selection, so the caret ends up moved by only one page
while the frame's own `scrollTop` (read live, inside each deferred callback) advances by the full count —
a real, observed divergence between the caret and the window it should be inside, caused entirely by the
test harness's own unrealistic pacing, not a bug in the editor. Spacing each dispatch with a short real delay
(`await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 15))` between presses) reproduces the real, sequential behaviour
correctly.

**The real `<textarea>` is never virtualized** — it still holds the whole file's text, confirmed live: a
20,000-line, 1.3 MB file's real `<textarea>.value.length` measured exactly `1300000` throughout, while the
rendered overlay (`.code-editor-highlight`) held only a few hundred run nodes and the gutter only the current
window's own `<span>`s — at the very top of a fresh file, 117 gutter numbers for a 20,001-"line" file (the
file's own trailing newline adds one degenerate final line, correctly counted); scrolling to the middle
(`scrollTop` set to roughly half of `scrollHeight`) correctly shifted the window to lines 9913–10117, with
the overlay's own `textContent` matching those exact lines byte-for-byte, not a placeholder; a jump straight
to a **never-before-visited** region at the very end of a different, never-touched 5,000-line file (no
intermediate scroll positions visited at all) landed the window at lines 4886–5001 with correct, byte-exact
content and no error — the "estimate, self-correct" trade-off settles instantly for a `nowrap` region since
every line is already known to be exactly one row; a wrapped file's own scrollbar proportions would be the
part that visibly settles, per the accepted design, though this specific test file's lines didn't wrap at
its default width. An in-window edit (a real `execCommand('insertText', ...)` three characters into the
middle of the file, at the caret) landed correctly in both the textarea and the overlay, confirmed via
`internal_clipboard`-free direct DOM reads, with the window only shifting afterward because of the browser's
own native "keep the caret visible" scroll nudge on an edit near the viewport's edge — not a spurious full
rebuild. **Undo across a window boundary**: with the window scrolled back to the very top (lines 1–117) and
the single edit still in the undo history, clicking **Undo** correctly reverted the text (confirmed via the
real `taLen`) and recentred the window all the way to lines 19899–20001 — because `UndoHistory`'s own
bootstrap snapshot (pre-existing, unrelated to this feature) puts the caret at the *end* of the file for the
very first history entry, and the window correctly followed it there, exactly as the render-time recenter
logic is supposed to. **Redo** brought the edit — and the window — back to lines 9984–10214, following the
restored caret once more. Real, correctly-paced `PageDown`/`PageUp` sequences (see the methodology note
above) moved the caret and kept the window following it at every step, including clamping cleanly at both
ends with no overshoot or error. The small-file regression check: a 5-"line" (6, counting the trailing empty
line) file rendered with the gutter and the overlay covering it exactly, byte-identical to the real
`<textarea>.value` — the window simply covers the whole file when it's smaller than the buffer, with no
virtualization artifact visible. The width-change/resize path (both narrowing and widening back) is covered
by the `ResizeObserver` bug and its fix above.

**Not separately exercised live in this pass**: `NoteEditPage.tsx`'s mirror-scroll (`onVisibleLineChange`)
and keyboard scroll-nudge (`onScrollNudge`) — both read only `frame.scrollTop`/`getComputedStyle(...).lineHeight`
directly, never anything about what's currently rendered in the DOM, so nothing about windowing the overlay or
gutter changes what they compute; the code paths themselves are unchanged from before this feature. A CPU
profile specifically isolating the keystroke cost on a huge file (as opposed to the DOM-node-count and
correctness checks above) wasn't separately captured this pass — the node-count reduction itself (a few
hundred run nodes and gutter spans instead of tens of thousands) is the direct, structural reason a keystroke
can no longer cost `O(file)` on this path, the same reasoning the earlier, now-superseded whole-document
incremental-measurement work already relied on for its own CPU-profile comparisons. `npx tsc --noEmit` and
`npm run build` both clean throughout; no Rust change, so `cargo test --lib` doesn't apply.

**Reported live, immediately after deploying the above: "if I navigate to the middle of the file, put the
cursor somewhere and type one letter, the image changes completely like I would have quickly scrolled away
instead of just typing a letter."** Reproduced exactly (a real markdown file, several thousand lines, scrolled
to the middle, caret placed there, one real keystroke) — two real, compounding bugs, both introduced by this
same virtualization feature, found live and fixed in sequence:

1. **The scroll position itself was jumping, on *every* keystroke, not just this one.** `fit()` (unchanged by
   this feature — it's always resized the real `<textarea>` by briefly setting its height to `0px` to
   re-measure `scrollHeight`, then restoring it) used to be harmless because, pre-virtualization, the
   highlighted `<pre>` sitting beside the textarea always held the *whole* file, so its own rendered height
   never depended on the textarea's momentary state. Once the overlay became windowed, it only had a
   `paddingTop` for the space *before* the window — nothing for the space *after* it — so `.code-editor`'s
   true scrollable height, during that one `0px` instant, collapsed down to roughly "the window's own
   position," far short of wherever the file was actually scrolled to, for any window short of the very end of
   the file. Reading `scrollHeight` forces that collapsed state to actually lay out, and the browser clamps
   `scrollTop` down to fit it, synchronously — a clamp that sticks even once the textarea's height is restored
   a moment later, since growing the box back doesn't restore a `scrollTop` the engine already overwrote.
   **Fixed** with a matching `paddingBottom` on the highlighted `<pre>` (`windowBottomPaddingPx`, `CodeEditor.tsx`:
   the pixel height of every line *after* the window, read from the same `gutterRowStarts.totalRows` the gutter
   itself now also exposes) — the element's own total height always equals the whole document's from then on,
   independent of whatever the textarea's own height happens to be at any given instant, including the one
   frame `fit()` spends at `0px`.
2. **That very fix broke something else**, found live immediately after deploying it, before trusting it: the
   gutter numbering for everything *before* the window started inflating, worse with every further keystroke
   — a 5,000-line file's own line ~2,400 reporting a cumulative row position around 7,200 after just a handful
   of edits. Root cause: `computeRowsPerLine` (`lib/wrappedLineRows.ts`) derives `totalRows` — used only for the
   window's own *last* line's row count — directly from `highlightEl.scrollHeight`, which, now that the element
   carries the new `paddingBottom` (and the pre-existing `paddingTop`), includes the padding too, not just the
   measured window's own content. The window's last line's row count (`totalRows - rowStarts[last]`) silently
   absorbed "everything after this window, compressed into one line's row delta" — and `carryForwardRows`
   (unaware anything was wrong) carried that one corrupted entry forward, uncorrected, on every subsequent edit,
   compounding further each time exactly where it had already been carried into the running gutter total.
   **Fixed** by having `computeRowsPerLine` read the element's own computed `paddingTop`/`paddingBottom` and
   work in one consistent coordinate system throughout: `contentTop` (where rows are measured *from*) is offset
   past the padding, and `totalRows` has both paddings subtracted back out — so the function measures only
   `value`'s own rows again, exactly as it did before either padding existed. A caller with no padding at all
   (the small-file, fully-covered-by-the-window case) is unaffected either way, since both paddings are then
   simply `0`.

Verified live (isolated **debug** data folder, desktop, real CDP, a real 5,000-line markdown file, real
`execCommand('insertText', ...)` keystrokes — reproducing the exact reported shape, not a synthetic shortcut):
scrolled to the file's own middle, caret placed there, one keystroke — `frame.scrollTop` measured bit-for-bit
identical before and after (`48768` both times), where the unfixed build's own first attempt (fix #1 alone,
before #2 was found) showed the gutter's own cumulative position for a line well before the window inflated to
roughly 3× what it should have been. With both fixes in place: nine more real keystrokes, spaced realistically
(400ms apart, past the debounce), left `scrollTop` unchanged throughout and the gutter's own spacing for the
window shown (lines 2414–2618) exactly `19.5px` apart at every single step — no drift, no compounding. The
width-change/resize path (both narrowing to 350px and restoring full width) was re-verified against this same
fixed build: wrapped correctly into 3 rows/line at the narrow width and back to 1 at full width, with the
*absolute* position of the line just before the window (`47061.5px`) identical in both directions — confirming
the padding-aware measurement composes correctly with the resize path too, not just the keystroke path. The
small-file case (a fresh 5-line file, window covering it entirely) was re-checked and remained byte-identical
to the textarea's own value, with both paddings correctly `0px`. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npm run build` both
clean throughout; no Rust change.

**Reported live again, after that fix had already been deployed: "it seems to work perfectly only as long as I
don't very scroll fast."** Asked directly what "doesn't work" actually looked like, since several rounds of
synthetic fast-scroll reproduction (large and small jumps, real CDP wheel bursts at various speeds, rapid
direction reversals, a CPU profile of a sustained fast scroll) all came back **correct** — the symptom turned
out to be nothing like a visual glitch during the scroll itself: **"the position where the caret lands when I
click on text is misaligned from the actual text... stays broken until the file is reopened."** That precise
description was what made it reproducible at all — clicking a specific, known character and comparing the
real caret position it landed at against the real textarea's own offset for that character, after a real, fast,
sustained wheel-scroll burst through a genuinely **wrapping** file (never reproducible on content that doesn't
wrap, since every line is trivially one row regardless of whether it was ever "measured" — confirmed directly).
Two distinct, compounding bugs, both pre-existing structural gaps the "estimate, self-correct" design's own
scope never actually covered, found and fixed in sequence:

1. **The accepted "estimate, self-correct" trade-off was scoped to the *scrollbar's* own proportions — never to
   where the overlay actually renders relative to the real `<textarea>`.** A line scrolled past quickly enough
   that its own window was never measured (the measurement is debounced 250ms after the *last* scroll event, so
   a sustained fast scroll can skip whole regions without ever settling into them) stays at its default "1 row"
   guess **forever**, until it happens to become a window's own content again — which a fast scroll specifically
   tends to skip. That default feeds directly into `gutterRowStarts.starts`, the running sum `paddingTop` is
   built from (above) — so an under-counted region *before* the window renders the overlay at the *wrong* pixel
   position relative to the real textarea underneath it, and since the two are supposed to sit exactly on top of
   each other (that's what makes a click land on the right character), a wrong `paddingTop` means **the browser
   maps the click to whatever the textarea's own, unrelated, correct layout has there — not to what the person
   is actually looking at**. **Fixed** with a new, hidden, out-of-flow `<pre>` (`measureScratchRef`, sharing the
   real overlay's own CSS class so font/width/wrapping match exactly, never shown, never part of the visible
   layout) used purely as a measuring stick: `measuredExtentRef` tracks one, single, ever-growing `[min, max)`
   line range that has had a *genuine* DOM measurement across a run of pure scrolling (reset the instant
   anything isn't a pure, same-text scroll — an edit, a width change, a line-height change — since those already
   have their own, separately-correct handling, and bridging across an *edit* would mean the gap's own line
   indices have shifted, which this simple min/max pair has no way to track). Whenever a new window doesn't
   already touch the known extent, the gap between them is measured too, in the hidden scratch element (nothing
   about the visible overlay or gutter ever shows the bridge happening), and the extent grows to cover both — a
   single island of known-correct rows that only ever gets bigger as scrolling continues, never fragmented. A
   gap bigger than `MAX_LINES_FOR_WRAP_MEASUREMENT` is **not** bridged (the same ceiling the whole feature
   already has for "not worth it" sizes) — the extent simply restarts at the new window, the same honest
   fallback a too-big file already gets everywhere else.
2. **The bridge fix above visibly *reduced* the misalignment but didn't eliminate it** — confirmed live, not
   assumed: a caret that used to land 16 lines away from the click landed less than one line away, consistently.
   Chased down to **two separate instances of the exact same class of bug**, both pre-existing in code this
   session's own earlier work had already touched (and, in one case, written) without ever noticing, because
   neither manifested visibly until a *wrapping* file was scrolled fast enough to expose them:
   - **The bridge's own gap slice** ended at `lineStarts[gap[1]]` — the character offset where the *next* real
     line (the window's own first line, or the old extent's) begins — which is exactly one character past the
     gap's own trailing `\n`. `computeRowsPerLine` reads a slice ending in `\n` as having one more, degenerate,
     zero-length trailing "line" of its own (its own documented, correct rule for the file's genuine trailing
     empty line) — and that fake entry's row count (always `1`) landed, one loop iteration later, at exactly
     `rows[gap[1]]`: the window's own real first line, silently overwriting whatever had just been correctly
     measured for it. **Fixed** by excluding that one trailing character from the slice handed to
     `computeRowsPerLine` — `gap[1]` is always a real, existing line (either the window's own start or the old
     extent's), so there's always a real "next line" to exclude the `\n` in front of, safely.
   - **The *pre-existing* main-window measurement had the identical gap, just never noticed**: `windowText =
     value.slice(windowStartChar, windowEndChar)` almost always ends in the `\n` separating the window's own
     last line from whatever comes after it, off-window — the *exact* same shape as the bridge's own bug above,
     just in code that predates the bridge (and predates even the `paddingBottom` fix) and had simply never been
     exercised enough to show it: before virtualization, `computeRowsPerLine` was only ever called on the *whole
     file*, where this only ever touched the file's own genuinely-empty trailing line — the one case the
     degenerate-line rule is actually correct and tested for. Confirmed directly, live, before touching the
     shared function: a hidden scratch `<pre>` given `"aaaa\nbbbb\n"` versus `"aaaa\nbbbb"` measured the
     *identical* `scrollHeight` either way — a trailing `\n`, in this engine's own `white-space: pre-wrap`
     rendering, adds **no row of its own** — so `computeRowsPerLine`'s existing, tested assumption that a
     degenerate trailing line sits at `totalRows - 1` (implying it occupies a separate row after the real
     content) silently undercounts the window's own *last real* line by exactly one row, every single time a
     window is measured, whenever that trailing `\n` is an artifact of *where the window happens to end* rather
     than the file's own genuine last line. Invisible before virtualization (there was only ever one window —
     the whole file); invisible even after it, on content that never wraps (every line is `1` row regardless of
     a one-row miscount). **Fixed without touching `computeRowsPerLine` itself** — its own degenerate-line rule
     is correct for what it was built and tested for — by having its *caller* (`measureWrappedRows`) exclude the
     window's own trailing `\n` from the slice it hands over, the identical fix as the bridge's, unless the
     window genuinely reaches the document's own end (in which case the existing, already-tested whole-file
     behavior is left completely alone).

Verified live (isolated **debug** data folder, desktop, real CDP, a real, genuinely wrapping markdown test file,
real `Input.dispatchMouseEvent`/`Input.dispatchMouseWheel` bursts — not synthetic DOM events, since the whole
point is whether a real click's own browser-native hit-testing agrees with where the overlay visibly puts the
text): the precise reproduction (fast-scroll down, fast-scroll back up, click immediately after a specific,
known line's own text) — before either fix, the caret landed on a *different line's own text, 16 lines away*
(3728 characters off); after the gap-slice fix alone, within one line (118 characters off, consistently); after
both fixes, the real caret offset matched the expected textarea offset **exactly**, byte for byte, confirmed
across three independent fast-scroll sequences landing on three different target lines. Re-verified as pure
regressions, with both fixes in place: the non-wrapping file (click-to-caret exact, as before), the small-file
case (byte-identical overlay/textarea, both paddings `0px`), and the width-change/resize path in both directions
(gutter spacing correctly proportional to the new wrap width, absolute position of the line before the window
unchanged by the resize). `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npm run build` both clean throughout; no Rust change.

## Typing after a fast scroll, and a blank editor after one

**Reported live, after the click-misalignment fixes above: "It works better. Although if I scroll very fast and
then stop, I start seeing a blank editor, If I then scroll just a little, I again see what I should... What is
still not acceptable is that when typing a letter (after scrolling very fast) all the coding highlight is
regenerated and I lose the scroll position — I see a totally different part of the document after I type a
letter (after a brief pause of not seeing anything)."** Two genuinely separate bugs, both living in the window's
own "jump to show something outside it" paths, plus a third, unrelated feature request handled in its own
section below.

**1. Typing after scrolling away flat-coloured the whole newly-revealed window, with a visible pause before the
real colours landed.** Confirmed live exactly as described: scroll a large file to its middle (the caret, never
moved, stays at offset 0 — nothing moves a textarea's own caret just by scrolling it), type one letter there —
`scrollTop` snaps from the middle straight to `0` (the render-time recentre, above, correctly revealing the
caret: this *jump* is standard, expected editor behaviour, every mainstream editor does it). The *landing* was
the problem: `resetPatcherIfWindowMoved`'s `controller.resetLiveState()` blanks `RunPatchController`'s own
`liveValue` to `null`, so the very next `applyApproximate` call sees an **empty** `oldValue` — its prefix/suffix
diff against the brand-new window's text finds nothing in common with nothing, so the *entire* window becomes
one flat, caret-coloured span, staying that way until the Worker's own debounced exact pass catches up
(`EXACT_DISPATCH_DEBOUNCE_MS` plus a whole-document re-tokenization — well over 200ms on an ordinary keystroke).
That flat flash *is* "not seeing" real content; the real colours landing a moment later then read as a second,
unrelated change — exactly "all the highlight regenerated... a totally different part of the document."

**Fixed by seeding the freshly-reset controller with a real, locally-highlighted tokenization of just the
window's own text, before `applyApproximate` ever runs for it** (`RunPatchController.rebuildWindow`, a thin
public wrapper around the existing private `rebuild`; `CodeEditor.tsx`'s approximate effect calls
`highlight(windowValue, effectiveLanguage)` — the exact same synchronous call `useBackgroundHighlighted`'s own
very first render already makes for a whole small file, now bounded to one window's worth of text instead —
then seeds the controller with `tokensOf()` of the result). `applyApproximate` then sees `oldValue` already
equal to `newValue` and does nothing further that commit: the seed *is* the visible result, correctly
syntax-highlighted from the very first paint. Not guaranteed byte-perfect at the window's own edges (a
multi-line construct that started before the window — an open fence, an unterminated comment — has no context
here to know about), but this is the same "estimate, then self-correct" trade this whole feature already makes
elsewhere: the next real exact pass patches over it the moment it lands.

**Deliberately scoped to an *edit* carrying the caret outside the window, not an ordinary scroll-driven shift**
(a new `windowMovedByEditRef`, set only by the render-time recentre, consumed and always reset by the
approximate effect): a fast, continuous scroll already triggers a window shift on nearly every qualifying
`scroll` event, and paying for one more synchronous `highlight()` call on *every one of those* would add exactly
the kind of main-thread work a fast scroll can least afford — a real risk of making bug 2 below worse, for a
case (plain scrolling) this fix was never asked to improve. An ordinary scroll-driven shift keeps its existing,
separately-accepted "flat colour until the Worker catches up" behaviour, unchanged.

**A second, independent bug shared the same trigger, found live while verifying the fix above, before trusting
it**: the render-time recentre computes `synthScrollTop` — the exact pixel position that would show the caret
— purely to *pick* a window via `computeWindowRange`, but **never once writes it to the real, visible
`frameRef.current.scrollTop`**. Confirmed directly: after the jump, the overlay's own content correctly
repositioned to line 0 (`paddingTop: 8px`, a `paddingBottom` covering everything past the window's own few
screens) — but `frame.scrollTop` stayed exactly where the person had scrolled it, meaning the *viewport* kept
looking at a pixel position that, relative to the newly-repositioned content, now fell squarely inside the
window's own trailing `paddingBottom`: real content exists, genuinely nothing is rendered at the position the
frame is actually scrolled to. Fixed by carrying the already-computed `synthScrollTop` from the recentre to the
same approximate effect (`pendingRecenterScrollTopRef`) and writing it to `frame.scrollTop` there, in the same
commit as the colour-seeding fix above, before the browser ever paints the mismatched state.

**2. "If I scroll very fast and then stop, I start seeing a blank editor… if I then scroll just a little, I
again see what I should" — a separate bug, with no typing involved at all, in `computeWindowRange`'s own
row-to-line lookup.** `computeWindowRange` picks a window by binary-searching `gutterRowStarts.starts` — the
per-line row-*start* array, built from each line's own row count, which for any line never yet scrolled into
view is the accepted "assume 1 row until measured" estimate (the module doc's own, confirmed trade-off). A fast
scroll through a long, heavily-wrapping, largely unmeasured stretch of a file can leave the *real* `scrollTop`
(always accurate — driven by the real, unvirtualized `<textarea>`, which lays out every line for real) far past
what the *estimated* `starts` array accounts for; `lastIndexAtMost` then has nothing left to find past the
array's own last entry and, correctly and faithfully, clamps to the very last line. **The common, unremarkable
shape of a text file — ending with a trailing newline — makes that last line a genuinely *empty* one**: the
clamped window ends up exactly one line long, and that one line has zero characters, so
`windowStartChar === windowEndChar` — an empty slice, nothing for `RunPatchController` to render, a genuinely
blank overlay.

**Confirmed live, not assumed**: a real, fast `Input.dispatchMouseEvent` wheel burst (`mouseWheel`, many ticks
with no inter-tick delay — a synthetic `scrollTop` assignment doesn't exercise the same under-measured-estimate
path a genuine fast scroll does) on a large, heavily-wrapping markdown file, checked immediately after the last
event: `pre.textContent.length === 0`, and **still `0` a full 1.3 seconds later with no further input** —
ruling out a transient render-lag race (which would have self-corrected within a frame or two) and matching
"stays blank until I scroll a little" precisely: nothing *else* re-triggers `computeWindowRange` until the next
`scroll` event does.

**Fixed with `widenIfCharsEmpty(range, lineStarts, totalChars)`** (`lib/wrappedLineRows.ts`, pure, Node-testable
like its siblings): widens a proposed window backward (decreasing `startLine`, clamped at `0`) until its
*character* span — not just its already-guaranteed-non-empty line-index span — covers at least one real
character. Applied at every `setWindowRange` call site (the render-time recentre, `handleScroll`'s own
hysteresis shift, and the mount-time correction effect) immediately after `computeWindowRange`'s own result,
before it's adopted. A pure, minimal fix for the *symptom* — never show literally nothing — without touching the
estimate/measurement machinery itself: the widened window shows whichever real, already-known lines sit just
before the degenerate tail, the same spirit as the already-accepted scrollbar-proportion trade-off, just
extended to guarantee *some* real content rather than *correctly positioned* content.

**A welcome side effect, confirmed live, not assumed from the code alone**: widening the window also hands the
*existing* gap-bridging mechanism (`measuredExtentRef`/`measureScratchRef`, from the click-misalignment fixes
above) something real to bridge *to* — a genuinely empty window gave it nothing productive to measure against,
but a widened one (even if still mispositioned relative to the real `scrollTop`) lets it measure the skipped
gap and correct `gutterRowStarts.starts` for the lines before it, which automatically repositions the *same*
window's `paddingTop` without needing the window's own line range to be re-chosen. Verified with a timed poll
(no further scroll input at all): a worst-case cold-file reproduction (first-ever fast scroll on a never-before-
measured, heavily-wrapping file) showed real but *badly mispositioned* content for roughly 400–500ms, then
self-corrected to exactly overlapping the viewport by the very next 100ms-spaced poll, and stayed correct for
every poll afterward — a short, bounded, self-healing window, not a permanent stuck state, and strictly better
than the un-widened case's indefinite, confirmed-still-blank-at-1.3s result.

Verified live (isolated **debug** data folder, desktop, real CDP throughout — real wheel bursts and real
`setTimeout`-deferred keystrokes, not synthetic events dispatched in a tight loop, which this project's own
history already warns reads nothing like real sequential input): the scroll-then-type reproduction, repeated
after each fix, landing with real syntax colours (`tok-keyword`/`tok-comment`/`tok-string`/`tok-function`) and a
correctly-synced `scrollTop` immediately, with the overlay's text byte-exact against the corresponding slice of
the textarea once the debounced exact pass settled; the fast-burst-to-near-end reproduction, repeated several
times from a fresh reload each time, never showing zero content again; a small file that fits entirely within
one window (byte-identical overlay/textarea, real colours, unaffected by any of this); and ordinary, in-window
typing at the end of an already-small, already-visible file (no window move triggered, `scrollTop` and content
both unchanged by the fix, confirming the edit-scoped gating doesn't fire where it shouldn't). `npx tsc --noEmit`
and `npm run build` both clean throughout; no Rust change, so `cargo test --lib` doesn't apply.

## Discarding unsaved changes

**Asked for in the same conversation as the two fixes above, as a third, separate item**: "regarding the
persisted unsaved changes: let's add a way to discard those unsaved changes." Every one of this app's three
text editors — the Files tab's own, Notes' File Manager/Note Files Explorer editor (`NotesApp.tsx`'s
`editing`), and the note editor (`NoteEditPage.tsx`) — already has **Save** and already shows "unsaved changes"
while dirty, but none had a direct way to throw the edits away and keep editing from a clean slate; the only
existing escape was leaving the editor (asked to confirm first) or, for a Filen file specifically, the
version-conflict dialog's own "Discard changes" choice. A plain **"Discard your unsaved changes"** button
(`RotateCcw`, beside Save) was added to all three, each asking first — the same destructive-action confirmation
this app's other direct "lose your edits" actions already use (closing an editor with unsaved changes is the
existing precedent in two of the three).

**`NotesApp.tsx` already had the right machinery, just not a direct way to reach it**: `discardEdits()` — drop
the edits and re-read the file from its source (Filen's own version, or in a branch, the branch's) — already
existed, but was only ever called from inside the version-conflict dialog, where choosing "Discard changes" is
itself the confirmation. `discardUnsavedChanges()` is a thin new wrapper: confirm, then call the exact same
function, so there's no second implementation of "throw the edits away" that could drift from the one the
conflict dialog already relies on.

**`NoteEditPage.tsx`** needed nothing re-fetched: `saved` already holds the last-known-good content (the file
as loaded, or as this same editor last wrote it) in memory the whole time `text` is being edited — `discard()`
is simply `setText(saved)` plus clearing the draft (`writeDraft(path, null)`), after confirming.

**`FilesTab.tsx` had no equivalent value to revert to at all** — its `editing` state was `{ path, content,
dirty }`, with no original kept once `content` was first edited (CLAUDE.md's own earlier "Text editors are now
dedicated pages, not popups" already noted this editor has no draft persistence either, unlike the other two).
Added a fourth field, `saved: string`, set alongside `content` at every point that represents a fresh load or a
successful save (opening a file, finishing a save, creating a new empty file) and left untouched by the
dirty-marking `onChange` path — the same role `NoteEditPage.tsx`'s own `saved` state already plays, just newly
introduced here rather than already present. `discardEditing()` confirms, then reverts `content` to `saved`.

Verified live (isolated **debug** data folder, desktop, real CDP, real native dialog interaction for each of
the two different confirmation mechanisms involved — not a DOM `.click()` alone, since the point is whether the
*real* dialog's own buttons do what's expected): in the **Files tab** (the admin-app, so its confirm is the
dialog plugin's own native box — `@tauri-apps/plugin-dialog`), a real `#32770` dialog's child buttons were
enumerated by handle and clicked with `BM_CLICK`: choosing **OK** reverted the editor's content to the
originally-loaded text byte-for-byte and correctly disabled the button again (`dirty` cleared); a second edit
followed by choosing **Cancel** left the edit completely untouched, confirming the destructive action only
fires on an actual confirmation.

**`NotesApp.tsx`'s own confirm (a *system app*'s, routed through `lib/dialogs` → the backend's
`confirm_dialog`) turned out to be a *different* native mechanism than first assumed** — not the webview-based
`system/prompt` window a page's own `alert`/`confirm`/`prompt` goes through (CLAUDE.md's "Prompts and
windows"), but its own plain native dialog (titled `"Notes" (or a page it shows) asks`, also a real `#32770`
window) with no CDP target of its own at all — confirmed by finding it had none even for the pre-existing,
completely untouched "Close without saving your changes?" confirm the same file already relies on, ruling out
anything specific to this new code. Found and clicked the same way as the Files tab's own dialog, once located
by Win32 window title instead of a CDP page list: choosing **OK** on a fresh edit correctly reverted the File
Manager editor's content to the original byte-for-byte and disabled the button; a second edit followed by
**Cancel** left it completely untouched — the identical round trip, through the real backend command, not
assumed from the shared code path.

`npx tsc --noEmit` and `npm run build` both clean for all three editors. **`NoteEditPage.tsx`'s own button was
not separately click-verified through its own native dialog** — its `discard()` is the same `setText(saved)` +
`writeDraft(path, null)` shape already proven correct in the Files tab, gated by the identical `confirm()` call
from `lib/dialogs` just proven correct against `NotesApp.tsx`'s own dialog above, so this rests on those two
already-verified halves rather than a third live click-through of the same mechanism.

## One more scroll-jump gap, coordinating the header with highlighting, and an option to disable highlighting entirely

**"Still if I scroll more than a few screens and then type, the view jumps all over the place."** Reported
again after every fix in "Typing after a fast scroll, and a blank editor after one" above had already
shipped — a genuinely different gap in the *same* mechanism, not a resurgence of any of the three already
fixed there. Reproducing it needed a specific shape the earlier reproductions hadn't tried: scroll to the
document's own *middle* (never an edge) and let the debounced wrap measurement actually **settle** there
first, *then* scroll further away, *then* type at the untouched caret (still at offset 0, since nothing had
moved it). Confirmed live, before any fix: `frame.scrollTop` stayed frozen at whatever the person had just
scrolled to while `gutterRowStarts`' own `paddingTop` silently jumped underneath it a few hundred
milliseconds later — once `wrapMeasure` (debounced, `WRAP_MEASURE_DEBOUNCE_MS`) finally landed for the
*new* window the render-time recentre had already picked. The render-time recentre itself only ever reads
`gutterRowStarts` to *choose* a window (via `computeWindowRange`) — it had no way to know the measurement
backing that choice was about to change again underneath it, since the measurement for a window that's
never been visited starts as the accepted "1 row" estimate and self-corrects once actually measured.

**Fixed with a second, narrower correction that waits for that exact measurement.** A new
`pendingRecenterCaretLineRef` is set, alongside the existing `pendingRecenterScrollTopRef`, in the same
render-time recentre block — the caret's own *line number*, not a pixel position, since the pixel position
is exactly the thing not yet trustworthy. A new `useLayoutEffect` keyed on `[wrapMeasure]` — firing the
moment a *new* measurement lands, whichever window it was for — re-reads the caret's line against the
*freshly*-measured `gutterRowStarts` and writes a corrected `frame.scrollTop` from that, then clears the
pending line so it only ever fires once per recentre (self-clearing; also cleared by `handleScroll`'s own
window-change branch or by a second recentre, whichever the person causes first by moving on). This is a
second correction on top of the first, standard, already-accepted "jump to show the caret" behavior every
mainstream editor has — not a new kind of jump, just making sure the one jump that does happen lands
correctly once the real numbers are in, rather than visibly drifting a second time once they are.

**Verified live** (isolated debug data folder — `-debug`, see "Folder layout" — never the production data
folder, confirmed throughout by checking only one process, `csdrive-webhost-tauriapp.exe` at
`Local\Ayran CsDrive WebHost\...`, stayed running the whole time; desktop, real CDP, the 40,000-line/
1,224,450-character synthetic `jump-test.js` reused from the earlier investigation): scrolled to 50% and let
it settle, then to 95%, caret placed back at offset 0 (focus-then-`setSelectionRange`, in that order — doing
it the other way around triggers the browser's own "scroll the caret into view," snapping `scrollTop` back
to 0 *before* the test ever starts, a methodology trap worth recording), then a real CDP keystroke (`a`) —
polled six times at 150ms intervals immediately afterward: `scrollTop` and the overlay's own `paddingTop`
read `0` and `"8px"` together, identically, at *every single poll*, with the real inserted character
(`afunction lineNumber0...`) and 558 real `tok-*` spans confirming genuine, correctly-positioned colored
content, not a blank or flat-colored window — no desynced moment was observable at any point this session's
own 150ms-resolution polling could catch, where the unfixed build (by the same reproduction, in the earlier,
already-recorded investigation) showed the two values plainly disagreeing for several hundred milliseconds.

**Also asked for in the same message: stop mixing the autohiding header's own CSS transition with the
editor's highlight-markup re-rendering — wait for one to finish before the other acts, "and vice versa."**
A new, small, module-level coordination point, `lib/editorHeaderSync.ts` (deliberately not React state or
context: a transition's own duration is a real-world timer running independently of any render, and at
most one header and one editor ever share a page in this app, so a plain flag plus a small subscriber list
is simpler than routing a one-to-one relationship through context). Four functions, two pairs:
`beginHeaderTransition`/`endHeaderTransition` bracket a header's own CSS transition (`HEADER_TRANSITION_MS`,
kept in step by hand with the transition durations in `App.css`/`notes.css`); `beginHighlightRerender`/
`endHighlightRerender` bracket `CodeEditor.tsx`'s own `RunPatchController` DOM patches. `runAfterHeaderTransition`/
`runAfterHighlightRerender` run a callback at once if the relevant side is already idle (the overwhelmingly
common case — both kinds of work are synchronous and fast, so by the time anything else checks, they're
almost always already done) or queue it for the moment the flag clears otherwise.

**Wired in both directions, matching "and vice versa" literally**: `lib/scrollAutohide.ts`'s `useScrollAutohide`
now routes every *actual* flip of `hidden` (never a call that would leave it unchanged) through
`runAfterHighlightRerender` first, then brackets the CSS transition it's about to trigger with
`beginHeaderTransition`/a timed `endHeaderTransition` — each deferred flip is tagged with a generation
counter so a newer scroll/resize decision silently supersedes a still-queued older one rather than both
firing out of order. `CodeEditor.tsx`'s own `handleScroll` now defers its window-range recompute via
`runAfterHeaderTransition` instead of calling `computeWindowRange`/`setWindowRange` inline, and both
`RunPatchController` `useLayoutEffect`s are bracketed with `beginHighlightRerender`/`endHighlightRerender`
(the first one skips its own bracket, along with the rest of its colouring work, while highlighting is
disabled — see below — but the scroll-follows-caret write inside it stays unconditional either way, since
it's the editor's own window-virtualization behavior, nothing to do with colouring).

**Verified live** (same isolated instance, same test file): a real `.editor-top-bar` header (shown, per its
own scroll-driven autohide) and a real wheel-scroll burst (`Input.dispatchMouseEvent`, ten ticks) down
through the editor correctly hid the header (`editor-top-bar-hidden` class present) while the content
scrolled correctly (`scrollTop: 4000`); scrolling back up correctly re-showed the header (`hidden: false`,
`scrollTop: 0`) with genuine, colored content still rendering underneath (855 real `tok-*` spans) — no
deadlock, no stuck-hidden state, and no visible fighting between the two coordinated mechanisms across
either direction of the scroll.

**The third part of the same message: "let's add an option to disable all code highlight, this should at
least enable a decent way of editing huge files."** A sixth editor setting, `editor.highlightDisabled`
(`lib/editorSettings.ts`, off by default, the same shared `global_settings` shape as `wrapLines`/
`lineNumbers`/etc., Settings → Text editors' own new checkbox: "Disable all code highlighting (a plain,
faster editor — line numbers still work)"). `useBackgroundHighlighted` takes a new `disabled` parameter:
checked even at mount (skipping the one, otherwise-unavoidable synchronous `highlight()` call this hook
makes on the whole file's text), skipped in the Worker-creation effect (no Worker at all — nothing
downstream would ever read its result) and in the dispatch effect (no approximate-colour bookkeeping either,
since `RunPatchController`'s own patches are what actually render it, and those are gated too). Both
`RunPatchController` effects early-return before their own colouring work when disabled — the first one
*after* the unconditional scroll-follows-caret write (see above: that part isn't highlighting-specific and
skipping it would silently reintroduce the "blank editor" shape this whole feature area already fixed once),
the second one (pure highlighting work throughout) right after `attachContainer`, so a reset to `null`
cleanly drops any live state with nothing further to apply. `measureWrappedRows` gained a matching early
return — the gutter's own wrap-aware row measurement exists solely to keep the highlighted overlay's line
numbers aligned with wrapped text the overlay no longer renders in this mode, so it falls back to the same
flat "one row per line" placement a file over `MAX_LINES_FOR_WRAP_MEASUREMENT` already gets. The JSX skips
rendering `.code-editor-highlight` and the hidden `measureScratchRef` measuring stick entirely when
disabled (`highlightRef.current` then reads `null`, which both effects' `attachContainer(null)` already
handles safely — resetting `RunPatchController`'s own live state with no container to patch into), and the
real `<textarea>` gets a new class, `code-editor-input-plain` (`App.css`: `color`/`-webkit-text-fill-color`
set to `var(--fg)`), since there's no colored copy left behind it to show through to — the usual
transparent-text arrangement would otherwise leave the box showing nothing at all.

**Verified live** (same isolated instance, through the real Settings UI — a real checkbox click, not a
